RE: Study Concerning the Relocation of the Grand Lodge Office
At the request of the Grand Master, a study was conducted to determine if the
relocation of the Grand Lodge office would result in cost savings to the Grand
Lodge of California. The Grand Master further asked that the study report the
financial impact of such a change to the related entities of the Grand Lodge.
The study has been completed and the following is a summary of the results.
Background
Since its formation, it has been the charter of the California Masonic Memorial
Temple to provide office space for the Masonic Homes of California and to the
California Masonic Foundation when it was formed. Since the Grand Lodge of
California has provided major financial and administrative functions of these
organizations, the California Masonic Code has stated that the corporate offices
of these organizations are located in the same office as the Grand Secretary of
the Grand Lodge of California. The Grand Lodge of California, therefore, has
been a tenant of the California Masonic Memorial Temple since its creation.
The Grand Lodge pays a pro-rated portion of the value of the lease of the third
floor offices to the CMMT.
Value of the third floor offices
Depending on the use of the offices, the third floor offices could be leased for as
much as $28 a square foot annually or for approximately $260,000.

GRAND LODGE F.& A.M. OF CALIFORNIA

57

Relocation Cities
Considering the services required by the Grand Lodge and its related entities,
five major cities were included in the study: Fresno, Sacramento, Riverside, Los
Angeles, and San Diego.
Major Factors of Consideration
While an exhaustive study could be made to determine all of the potential
savings and related expenses of such a relocation, the two major factors were
determined to be the cost of leasing and new office space and the savings in
labor costs in different markets. If after looking at these two major factors, there
was an indication that a relocation might result in cost savings, the greater
expense for further study would be recommended.
Office Space Lease Expenses
Using various commercial real estate Internet listing services, the average price
per square foot (P/SF) price was determined for each location for similar type
space. The average P/SF in the cities in the studied ranged from $15 in Fresno to
$22 in Los Angeles and San Diego. A placeholder figure of $24K annually was
added in expense for utilities. The study finds that the total additional expense to
the Grand Lodge and its related entities for the lease of alternate office space
would range from $324K - $464K annually. See the attached spreadsheet for
details.
Savings in Labor Expenses
Wages in San Francisco are higher than in any other city in the state. The cost of
living is higher, also. Using the cost of living calculator and related wage
adjustment calculator on the web site www.salary.com, the labor savings were
calculated for each city. The savings range from 8% in Sacramento to 14% in
Riverside. The study finds that the total savings to the Grand Lodge and its
related entities would range from $176K to $308K annually. See the attached
spreadsheet for details.
Total Savings
Considering the two factors, there is no savings to the Grand Lodge and its
related entities on the whole in any of five cities. The total additional expense
ranges from $38K to $268K.

58

PROCEEDINGS OF THE

Effect on the Related Entities
Assuming the charter of the CMMT remains the same, it would bear the greatest
burden of a relocation of the Grand Lodge office. The additional costs to the
CMMT (which include extra labor that it would require at the San Francisco
site) range from $371K to $497K. Assuming that CMMT could lease the third
floor for $252K, the organization is still at a serious loss. The additional costs
to the Grand Lodge range from $20K to $49K per year. Please see the attached
spreadsheet for details.
Summary
Based on the two major factors of relocated the Grand Lodge office, it is not
recommended that the Grand Lodge go to the time and expense of a more
extensive study of a relocation of the Grand Lodge office. CMMT is able to
provide office space to the Masonic Homes of California and the California
Masonic Foundation in its San Francisco facility, as it is chartered to do, in such
a manner that the affect of reduced labor expenses is negated entirely.

GRAND LODGE F.& A.M. OF CALIFORNIA

59

Strategic Planning
One of the lessons we learned from our ancient brothers is the need for
a plan. A great building starts with a plan. The plan provides the vision for the
future that allows us to prepare the ground and lay the foundation for future
generations to complete the building. Grand Lodge has developed a plan for
future health of our fraternity. As part of the development of the strategic plan
that was adopted at the annual communication, the Executive Committee
worked on the initiatives to implement the strategic plan. These initiatives are
the detailed drawings that give the plan form and make it possible to be
completed. While these were adopted for 2005 it is the responsibility of the
Grand Master to establish the initiatives for each successive year.
2005 STRATEGIC INITIATIVES
I. Membership
• Member Retention
• Prospect cultivation and relations
• Candidate advancement
II. Lodge Development
• Lodge revitalization process
• New lodge development strategy
III. Masonic Knowledge
• Education
• Improved candidate and member education
• Increased and enhanced statewide educational events
• Programs to encourage new Masonic research and
writings
• Increased emphasis on applied Freemasonry
• Relevant educational resources for Masons, their
families, and communities
• Ritual
• Improved approach to instruction
• Impressive conferral of degrees
• Advanced technology for lecture delivery
• Recognition of outstanding ritual work
IV. Leadership Development
• Comprehensive training program
• Leadership identification and cultivation
• Increased awareness of leadership opportunities
• Lodge officer development strategy

GRAND LODGE F.& A.M. OF CALIFORNIA

61

V. Masonic Center Development and Preservation
• Masonic property standards
• Standardization of center association business practices
• Masonic center preservation and capital planning
program
• Preservation and utilization of historic sites
• Preservation of historic artifacts
VI. Communications
• Quality communication tools and improved websites
• Expanded resources for lodges
• Expanded distribution of Masonic information
• Expanded distribution of the California Freemason
VII. Community Involvement
• Community program development
• Increased participation in community events
• Effective use of Public Ceremonies at community events
VIII. Masonic Relief
• Increased care for Masons, widows, and their families
• Increased participation in Masonic Philanthropies
• Lodge support for other charitable programs
• Individual involvement in community relief
Membership Statistics
The fiscal year in membership ended on June 30. For the fourth
consecutive year, our lodges have conferred more degrees than in the previous
year. As a matter of fact, our lodges conferred more degrees in 2005 than in any
year since 1993. You will notice however, that more degrees were conferred
per lodge in 2005 than in 1993. Considering lodge activity, in 2005 more
degrees per lodge were conferred and more Master Masons per lodge were
raised since 1984. By these important measures, it is fair to say that 2005 was
the best year in membership activity in 21 years.

GRAND LODGE F.& A.M. OF CALIFORNIA

63

These membership statistics show that over the last five years that
lodges are attracting more new members. Our efforts to make our fraternity
more meaning to our members and our communities is working. If we can
continue these trends we will stop our declining membership sooner than
predicted and begin to grow again.
Our fraternity has much to offer our members, our communities, and
our country. During these times of intolerance, hatred and greed, our values are
needed more than ever. Each of us has taken an obligation to improve ourselves
by a commitment to lifelong learning of the meaning of our values. By gaining
more light in Masonry each of us become a beacon that shines the light of
freemasonry. By sharing this light we make our communities a better place for
everyone to live.
It has been an honor and a privilege to serve you as your Grand Master.
Thank you for the opportunity to share the light of freemasonry.
Respectfully submitted,
David R. Doan, Grand Master

APPENDIX B

GRAND LODGE F.& A.M. OF CALIFORNIA

81

REPORT OF THE DEPUTY GRAND MASTER
2005-2006 Grand Lodge Budget
Frederick L. Sorsabal, Deputy Grand Master
Each year the Deputy Grand Master is required by Section 7040 of the
California Masonic Code to prepare and submit at the Annual Communication
of the Grand Lodge, the budget for the period of November 1, 2005 through
October 31, 2006. Again this year, we are presenting a balanced budget for
your consideration and approval. This budget has been reviewed by the Finance
Committee and Grand Treasurer. Both have recommended approval of the
attached budget.
This year’s Budget is based on the amount of per capita collected this
year plus additional income from the interest on short-term investments;
accumulated earnings from the Grand Lodge Endowment Fund; anticipated
degree fees; a grant from the California Masonic Foundation; and
reimbursements from other Masonic Entities for services provided to them by
Grand Lodge.
Our commitment to our constituent lodges remains the same. We have
reduced spending where possible without reducing the effectiveness of the
organization which serves these lodges. The slight increase in some expenditure
is primarily the cause of inflation and the rise in our insurance rates. The Grand
Master and the Executive Committee are committed to finding other revenue
sources to assist in our ability to present to the Craft a balanced budget.
Sacrifices in all areas are inevitable.
The 2005-2006 Grand Lodge Budget is funded by the Per Capita of
$26 which was adopted at the last Annual Communication and which the lodges
are in the process of paying. This is based upon 68,816 Master Masons in good
standing on the rolls of our lodges as of June 30 of this year. The California
Masonic Foundation provided a grant of $86,000 to assist in funding the
continuation of Masonic Education and Leadership Training.
Budgeted expenses have increased slightly over last year’s expenses.
This is due primarily to inflation, increased Lodge Insurance premiums, and
increased fees for the Annual Audit. The total budgeted expenses are
recommended at $2,014,800 and include those increases already noted along
with $120,350 for the publication of the California Freemason magazine which
is a fine communication tool to the members of the Craft. I am intending to
continue the programs that implement the Strategic Plan and along with the
grant by the California Masonic Foundation, provide enhanced Masonic
Education, Leadership Training and Membership Developmment. These
programs are essential to the needs of our Fraternity if it is to remain a premier
fraternal organization in our State.

82

PROCEEDINGS OF THE

I have shown the salaries of the Grand Secretary and Assistant Grand
Secretary under the General and Administrative Accounts. The figure on the
right hand column is the net figure supported by the Per Capita. It must be
pointed out that these two positions are responsible, as administrators to all the
entities of Grand Lodge. The costs for these two positions are offset, in large
part by reimbursements from other Masonic entities, through our Corporate
Services funding. It should also be noted that there are a total of 6.75 full time
equivalent (FTE) positions that work directly for Grand Lodge.
It is my hope that the enclosed pages are self-explanatory and provide
you with a better understanding of the Grand Lodge budget.

PROCEEDINGS OF THE

INSTALLATION ADDRESS
FREDERICK L. SORSABAL
Grand Master of Masons in California, 2005-2006
Good morning. I want to thank you for participating and sharing this
wonderful day with the 2006 Grand Lodge family. It is a great honor and a
wonderful privilege for me to be elected the 141st Grand Master of Masons in
California. It is truly humbling and with it goes an awesome responsibility,
which I hope I will be able to carry out as well as my predecessors have.
I also want to express my gratitude to my family and friends for
attending, some of whom have come from a long distance to witness this
ceremony. To my wife, I want to express my love for her understanding for all
these years in providing me with the opportunity to be very active in the family
of Freemasonry. She truly is my co-pilot and is very supportive of the values of
our craft. Without her understanding and assistance, I would not have been able
to devote so much time to the craft. I really appreciate her forbearance and
help!
To the most worshipful David Doan and his wife Teri, I want to
express my gratitude and thanks for the outstanding representation they gave to
Grand Lodge this last year, and in providing outstanding leadership to our grand
jurisdiction. As you know, it is difficult to carry on the responsibilities of Grand
Master and continue with full time employment, particularly as Deputy Chief of
Police of the City of Los Angeles. To both of you, my thanks for your
continued support of our craft and your devotion to the Grand Lodge of
California, plus being truly good friends to Marlene and me. A job well done!
My gratitude to the order of DeMolay will forever be carried in my
heart for it was in DeMolay that I was to learn the skills of leadership that were
used in my professional career and in my advancement in the family of
Freemasonry. It is a debt that will be hard to repay. Our youth orders have the
necessary value system and training to provide the future leaders of our craft
with the essential background and skills to carry us forward. We must endeavor
to continue to give them the needed support (both financial and manpower) so
that their orders will succeed.
My brothers and friends, the theme for the 2005-2006 Grand Lodge
year is: "United We Stand, Together We Can Make A Difference, The Family
of Freemasonry". In order for our Masonic family to be vital in our
communities, it will be necessary for each of our lodges, chapters, councils,
valleys, shrine centers and courts to walk in lock step and show our values of
brotherly love, relief, truth, along with temperance, fortitude, prudence, and
justice. But if these values are not seen and practiced, how will the community
know what we stand for? Together, we will reach out in strength to lead our

GRAND LODGE F.& A.M. OF CALIFORNIA

91

communities and to bond together so that all may see the ideals of the family of
Freemasonry.
In the Farmer’s Almanac for 1823, it was published in an article
entitled: “Character of a Freemason”; the real Freemason is distinguished from
the rest of mankind by the uniform unrestrained rectitude of his conduct. A
Freemason would be just if there were no laws, human or divine except those
written in his heart by the finger of his creator. In every climate, under every
system of religion, he is the same. He kneels before the universal throne of God
in gratitude for the blessings he has received and humble solicitation for his
future protection. He venerates the good men of all religions. He disturbs not
the religion of others. He restrains his passions, because they cannot be
indulged without injuring his neighbor or himself. He gives no offense, because
he does not choose to be offended. He contracts no debts which he is certain he
cannot discharge, because he is honest upon principle.” Values written about
our craft more than 180 years ago and which are true today.
As Masons, we must again put our arms around all our appendant and
concordant bodies, as each of these groups must have Masonic heritage to exist.
We must never forget that the symbolic lodge is the core of the family of
Freemasonry. Each must realize where they started and in order to continue, we
must support and assist each other. Once this is accomplished, we can go out
into our communities as missionaries to practice our good works. Let us
embrace each other and not continually disagree. These disagreements have
provided a tremendous schism within our ranks to the detriment of our work.
Longfellow wrote: “all your strength is in your union, all your danger is in
discord.” We must cooperate with one another, and build a useful strategy for
the future. I challenge each of our orders in the family of Freemasonry to come
together so that we may again assist each other in providing society with a
positive pathway for life. We can then become emissaries of those great lessons
that may awaken in others the desire to seek our light. It is the powerful
message we deliver which transcends politics, religion and social stature. The
message that each of our orders carry is as valid and needed today as it was
centuries ago. We must remember that united, we work together in a diversity
of ways to achieve one goal. Divided, we weaken the whole of Freemasonry
and lessen the effectiveness of each separate part.
Masonry possesses the manpower, augmented by affiliated
organizations, to exert a beneficial influence throughout our society. The
teachings of Masonry lies dormant in too many of our members. We must
encourage every man who has knelt at the Masonic altar to rise daily with a
consciousness of tasks about him, and by precept and example, strive for that
true brotherhood of man under the fatherhood of God which we cherish so
fervently. Then and only then, will those who observe us realize that the future
peace and good will of our society depends on the philosophy taught in our
lodge rooms.

92

PROCEEDINGS OF THE

My brothers, we must recognize that change is the only constant in life.
The way we did things 100 years ago, certainly are not done that way today.
Masonry must also look at how and why it does things today. Our basic tenents
will never change, but our practice of them may. The changes we have seen
require different use of our talent and our time. Either we adapt ourselves to
these changing times or we will no longer exist. The same holds true of our
great fraternity. No one is asking Masonry to change – only that it adapts to
today's and tomorrow's world. We must change our approach to, and visible
exemplification of, that which is good. Allen Roberts, in his book, “The Craft
and Its Symbols”, expresses this thought very well. He says, “The principles of
Freemasonry are transmitted unimpaired, but the vehicle through which they are
expressed changes as the years go on.” We need to continue to change our way
of imparting the teachings of Freemasonry to those who will seek them, and in
so doing, it is within the limits of probability that we will find new ways to
attract worthy, well-qualified men to apply for membership in our fraternity.
Some of you will say, “if it ain't broke, don't fix it.” Expressed another way, “if
you wish to keep getting what you are now getting, keep doing what you are
now doing.” In order to meet the needs of the younger men who are coming
into our craft, we need to adapt new ways of reaching out and providing the
necessary programs in our lodges to peak their interests and retain their
membership.
Longfellow has said “look not mournfully at the past – it comes not
back again; wisely improve the present – it is thine; go forth to meet the
shadowy future without fear and with a manly heart.” Shouldn't this be our
determination today?
As you can see one of the strategic initiatives we will be working on
this coming year is membership development and retention. Retention is so
important and I ask each of you to go back to your lodge and take a critical
review of what you are doing to retain your present members and getting new
ones.
Another strategic initiative this year will be to enhance the leadership
skills within each lodge. To be successful, we must have good leaders at the
grass root of our lodges who have vision, and a willingness to work. We need a
base of younger men who speak out and are not “put in their place” by us older
Masons. We need to focus our attention upon the young men in our lodges by
giving them increased knowledge and an opportunity to become participants and
not mere spectators. We must provide programs within our lodges for the
diverse generations we have and will always have in our Order. Interests must
fit both our elder statesmen and our newer, younger members. However, we
must always remember that the future rests with these younger men. A great
leader will see what will work in his lodge. Any organization is but the
lengthened shadow of its leaders. Our leaders will provide necessary goals and
programs for our lodges to attract and retain its members. Leaders will

GRAND LODGE F.& A.M. OF CALIFORNIA

93

remember to make their lodge meetings more than just boring repetition of our
ritual or degrees. He will provide an avenue for the family to enjoy our
Masonic heritage. A great leader will bring fun into the lodge (not tom foolery),
making sure we enjoy our family and the fellowship of our craft, but also
placing a focus on the future of Freemasonry.
Grand Lodge can only assist if our constituent lodges participate in the
leadership programs provided. Recognizing that finances may be a factor in
attendance at these sessions, I am willing to provide, through the generosity of
the California Masonic Foundation, “scholarships” for officers to attend the
Retreats Grand Lodge presently provides. These scholarships will be available
to lodges who have not sent an officer to our leadership training programs in the
last five years. They will be evenly divided north and south; Senior and Junior
Wardens. Further information will be sent to those lodges who have not sent an
officer to our leadership retreats. We must take our leadership vacuum
seriously.
It is my belief that by having leadership skills, which are developed in
Grand Lodge's leadership training programs, and practiced in our lodges, we
will begin to see fewer of our lodges wanting to consolidate. The prime reason
this has happened, in my opinion, is that the lodge has the necessary members,
but no one wants to step up and become its leader. Time and skill is essential to
halt, what I call the bleeding of our presence in our communities.
Another initiative I would like to see carried on again this year is that
of Masonic education or knowledge. It is my hope to promote a culture of
Masonic formation, both the practical as in applied Freemasonry (how you carry
out our teachings in your daily life), and more esoteric as it might apply to
Masonic writings. Masonic formation, education, and knowledge are keys by
which members benefit from this great fraternity. Through Masonic formation,
we develop the skill we need to take our current state – represented by the rough
ashlar – and transforming it into something better – represented by the smooth
ashlar. Masons search for further light – a light that is knowledge of our world
and our place in it. Our Masonic light is obtained through introspection, study
and education. Our identity as Masons must include work on ourselves both as
individuals and as a brotherhood. Our decline in membership over the past 50
years is merely a symptom of the loss of Masonry’s relevance to our lives and
our communities. Our focus on the past has blinded us to the challenges of the
present. And it is the present that we must address both as individuals and as a
fraternal organization. Our reliance on former brothers’ successes has
weakened our commitment to achieving our own Masonic identities. Therefore,
we will be establishing a fund through the California Masonic foundation,
entitled The Melt Fund, Masonic Education and Leadership Training, by which
I hope to provide the resources so that these teachings may be carried out in the
future without stressing the per capita which each lodge now provides. This
fund has already started as a Masonic widow, who wants to remain anonymous,

94

PROCEEDINGS OF THE

has provided $50,000 which she has challenged our lodges to at least match.
She knows how important Masonic formation was to her husband and is to the
future of our craft. She has invested in the future of our fraternity and has
challenged our lodges to do the same. Marlene and I are asking that, if you
desire, when we come to your lodge that a contribution be given to this fund
instead of personal gifts to us. You and your lodge will then become a very
important part of providing enhanced Masonic formation throughout our
jurisdiction.
I am encouraging Masons to visit other lodges during the year.
Visiting and meeting Masons in different lodges is an outstanding way to see
how other lodges carry out their programs. To this endeavor, I am asking the
lodges to provide Masonic passports so that a brother may proudly show where
he has been and which lodges he has visited. This was carried out a few years
ago, but somehow discontinued. What a shame for it is an outstanding way to
see Masonry in action throughout our jurisdiction. Passports are now available
through the supply office of Grand Lodge and I would ask that your lodge
participate. I would encourage each of our lodges to provide passports to your
newly raised Masons.
You probably are asking why I picked the beehive as our logo for this
year. It is a very important Masonic emblem, now left out of our lecture. It is a
symbol of industry and cooperation. A symbol of social obligation; reciprocal
love and friendship – social obligation as a part of the symbol is seen in the
harmonious working together of many units to a single end and reciprocal love
and friendship in the fact that in the most crowded hive, bees live in peace and
harmony with their fellows.
A bee is a hard and tireless worker, not for himself but for the swarm.
It works in complete cooperation and does so without dissension and lives in a
society ruled by order. Man must work as a unit to accomplish great things.
The builders of old worked as a unit to build those great cathedrals. He could
not work alone and expect to build the mighty edifice.
So in conclusion we, as Masons, must imitate the bee, be industrious,
work with others and for others, take pride in our vocations, obey the rules of
our society and strive to add to our body of knowledge and understanding.
Otherwise we are useless members of society.
Remember, United We Stand, Together We Can Make A Difference,
The Family of Freemasonry. Thank you again for participating in today's
ceremonies. You have truly humbled me.

GRAND LODGE F.& A.M. OF CALIFORNIA

97

REPORT OF THE SENIOR GRAND WARDEN
Per Capita Recommendation for 2006-2007
Melvyn B. Stein, Senior Grand Warden
It is my responsibility as Senior Grand Warden to propose a per capita
at the 2005 Annual Communication to provide funds to meet the 2006-2007
Grand Lodge Budget. Substantial time and effort has already been expended in
a line by line review and projection of membership and expenses in reaching the
seventh draft of the budget itself.
The present system of budget projection is both comprehensive and
exhaustive and begins a full four years before a budget is recommended to
Grand Lodge at the Annual Communication. The proposed budget is carefully
tracked and adjusted as each year unfolds. That means that the budget upon
which the per capita is based is as accurate in forecasting our needs as is
logically possible.
Accordingly, a budget for 2006/2007 has been developed which will
support current programs, and reflect our projected membership as well as
increasing insurance and other costs. That budget will require a per capita of
$27.50. The Grand Lodge Finance Committee, Grand Secretary, Grand
Treasurer and Director of Finance met at length with me to review the budget
projections for 2006/20007, and all urged and agreed that this is the amount that
is needed to support our ongoing programs.
Preliminary charts are attached showing that approximately ¾ of the
proposed per capita has been designated to be spent on services to members,
lodges and Inspectors. The per capita takes into consideration a reduction in
membership resulting in a loss of over $89,000 in income while at the same time
increases expenses less than a minimum three (3%) percent for cost of living.
Strong, active lodges are necessary to assure the success of the
strategic plans of both the Grand Lodge and individual lodges as well as attract
and retain quality members. Much of the 2006/2007 Budget is committed to
maintaining our present course in supporting leadership training, Masonic
education and lodge outreach. The proposed per capita of $27.50 will meet the
2006/2007 Grand Lodge balanced budget requirements.
I look forward to working with each of you and the other officers and
members of your lodge as Masonry continues to be a strong and positive force
for the benefit of our brethren and our communities.

105

REPORT OF THE GRAND TREASURER
To the Most Worshipful Grand Lodge, F.& A.M. of California:
In keeping with a long standing custom, this, my last published report
to you as Grand Treasurer will, of necessity be rather lengthy. A shorter version
was delivered at the Annual Communication.
The annual audited financial statements for the Grand Lodge of
California and its entities (Masonic Homes of California, California Masonic
Memorial Temple, California Masonic Foundation), the Retirement Plan and all
related trust accounts are published in their entirety in the Proceedings. I once
again urge you to take the time to review these important financial statements
and become familiar with the fiscal operations of this, your Grand Lodge. Your
review will confirm the considerable extent and complexity of our organization
and that the final and complete audits run to more than 50 pages. I would ask
that as you review the audits, you refer to the nine sections (I – IX) as listed
below.
As your treasurer, and on behalf of the financial staff, I would like to
share nine significant areas of activity that have been identified, undertaken,
completed, or substantially addressed during the past year. Each of the nine
areas clearly identify and define improved and strengthened financial
accountability, with ever greater transparency within the Grand Lodge and its
several entities.
I.

Financial Staffing:


In October 2004, we added a professional Controller to the financial
staff of Grand Lodge. This position brings technical expertise in
general accounting and a wealth of experience in audit and tax
preparation. The Controller’s responsibility is to assure that all
accounting for the Grand Lodge, and related entities, is completed and
recorded with the highest integrity, accuracy and transparency, all in
strict compliance with generally accepted accounting principals and
governing rules.



We have also restructured other financial department services staff
positions and assignments to better serve Grand Lodge requirements.
We centralized payroll processing which has created a higher degree of
professionalism and efficiency.



In the coming months, we will perform an in depth evaluation of the
purchasing function, as we continue to assess the strengths and skills of
our staff to ensure professional performance.

PROCEEDINGS OF THE

106
II.

Support for Insurance Programs:


We continued to work effectively with our insurance broker to improve
the Grand Lodge and related Masonic entities insurance coverage at an
effective ratio of risk to cost/benefit.



As you were advised, the renewal dates for insurance coverage was
transitioned to April to facilitate annual budget preparation. (For
ninety percent of our organizations, the deadline for finalizing required
budget data is the end of April.)



In August 2004, the Insurance Committee developed an insurance
program for the Hall Associations which provides improved coverage
at greatly reduced premiums.
The considerable support and
administration required for this program is the responsibility of the
Finance Department.



With the August 2005 renewal of the Hall Association insurance
program, Director’s & Officer’s coverage and molestation protection
were added to provide more comprehensive coverage.



We continue to communicate and work closely with our lodges and
hall associations to ensure adequate and appropriate insurance
coverage. This takes many forms, from acting as an internal resource,
to assisting the Insurance Committee’s presentation to various
gatherings of lodges and Masonic associations.

III.

Workflow Analysis:


IV.

We have now completed the first phase of a Financial Workflow
Analysis (FWA). This initial phase of the FWA included three areas:
the resident asset assignment process; accounts payable/check
processing; and accounts receivable/cash receipts. With this FWA
completed, we now have in-place documented, high-level procedures
and internal controls for these processes. In addition, we have
implemented numerous changes in our procedures to insure appropriate
internal controls and produce more accurate, transparent and timely
financial information.
Financial Reporting:



Over the past year we have redesigned all financial reports for the
Grand Lodge and its entities. As you know, the Finance Department
generates separate, detailed monthly operating reports for all
organizations. In addition, separate and detailed quarterly reports are
generated for the Grand Lodge committees and the Masonic Homes is

GRAND LODGE F.& A.M. OF CALIFORNIA

107

provided quarterly balance sheets and capital project reports. All of
these reports have been redesigned and updated to make them more
readable, understandable, transparent and to include more useful
management, governance and operating data. As an example, the
Masonic Homes now receive monthly cost-per-day reports based on an
in-depth analysis completed earlier in the year. This valuable
comparative measure of operating expenses, including depreciation is
an important management tool.


V.

We continue to fine-tune the content and presentation of both the
Grand Lodge budget and per capita analysis for the Grand Master and
for distribution to the general membership. We now include more
detailed information in an improved format.
Annual Audits:



Audits of the Grand Lodge and all related entities were completed as
per schedule. All were given a clean, unqualified auditor’s opinion.



The IRS recently completed an audit of The Grand Lodge Retirement
Plan. This was a random audit not generated by any problems with the
tax returns as filed. The audit was completed with no audit
adjustments.



The Finance Department together with the Grand Lodge Audit
Committee (see VII below) completed a request for proposal, analysis,
evaluation and selection of a new accounting firm. Moss Adams was
hired to replace PricewaterhouseCoopers who declined to quote due to
inability to continue to provide the level of service required.

VI.

Annual Budgets:


Annual budgets for the Grand Lodge and all related entities (a total of 4
independent and comprehensive budgets) were completed efficiently
and on time. All budgets were, without exception, balanced within the
constraints of available revenue.



We continue to review and evaluate all financial expenses. Through
effective management, we were able to cut expenses in a number of
areas, including bank charges and insurance.

VII.

Changes in Regulatory Environment:


The State of California enacted new laws governing the organizational
structure and financial procedures of non-profits, such as our entities.

PROCEEDINGS OF THE

108

These new laws required the Grand Lodge and related entities to
restructure the governance model. Your Grand Lodge elected to
comply with both the letter and spirit of the law. Accordingly, the
Grand Master established an Audit Committee to oversee the financial
functions of the Grand Lodge and related entities.


The Audit Committee members also serve as the Grand Lodge Finance
Committee as prescribed by the California Masonic Code.



The same Committee also serves as the Retirement Plan Trustee for the
defined benefit pension plan, again as prescribed by the California
Masonic Code.

VIII.

Use of Scarce Resources:



The Finance Department has provided critical expense and contract
review functions for the various entities we serve. Staff was successful
in renegotiating the Union City elevator maintenance agreement to
reduce cost and provide new and improved service throughout the
facility. More than $12,000 in annual savings was realized through this
process.



We provide similar negotiating and service reviews for other areas of
operating, including cell phones, office supplies, dietary supplies,
medical supplies, etc. to generate further savings and to enhance
service levels and quality.



We are in the midst of a full review of all medical service provider
contracts to update these contracts and renegotiate appropriate rates.

IX.

Investments:


A professional staff member was hired to internally oversee and report
on the investment portfolio of the Grand Lodge and related entities.
This professional brings many years of experience in investment
oversight, reporting and analysis to ensure the accuracy, transparency
and protection of the investment assets.



The Grand Lodge Investment Committee continues their thorough and
active oversight role for the actual investments and the investment
portfolio performance. This staff position allows the Investment
Committee and the several investment managers, selected by the
Committee, to better use their time in active management of the
investment funds and to position the portfolio for long term growth and
protection of the assets entrusted to them.

GRAND LODGE F.& A.M. OF CALIFORNIA

109

As the above would confirm, this has been a challenging, exciting and
productive year for your Grand Treasurer and for the Finance Department staff
and they look forward to another year of service to this great fraternity. For my
part, I look forward to retirement, in the knowledge that the Grand Master and
Executive Committee have selected and will recommend to you a highly
qualified, dedicated and committed replacement. I wish him every success in
what I found to be one of the most rewarding of assignments within this Grand
Lodge.
My sincere appreciation goes to the Grand Master, Grand Secretary,
Assistant Grand Secretary, the Director of Finance and the entire Finance
Department. You made a challenging assignment a pleasure and for your
dedication and commitment to excellence, I shall forever be indebted.
Respectfully submitted,
Warren J. Blomseth, PGM, Grand Treasurer

113

REPORT OF THE GRAND SECRETARY
This is my fourteenth report to you as Grand Secretary. First, I wish to
express appreciation to our Grand Master, and to the other members of the
Executive Committee, for the support given to your Grand Lodge Office this
past year. The Grand Lodge Office is the hub of your Grand Lodge, and we
have striven to deliver the best services possible to the leadership of this Grand
Lodge, to all Grand Lodge Officers, all boards and committees of Grand Lodge,
to our inspectors, and to our individual lodges. To the extent that we have made
their tasks easier, we have succeeded.
The Grand Secretary is effectively the Chief Administrative Officer of
Grand Lodge. As such his duties are many and varied. Under the overall
direction of the Grand Master, he is responsible for the administrative activities
of the Grand Lodge, which go far beyond supervision of the Grand Lodge
Office itself. He serves as secretary to three constitutional boards – the Homes
Board, the California Masonic Foundation, and the California Masonic
Memorial Temple. While the Homes has its own executive staff, the Grand
Secretary has significant responsibilities for the Homes, not the least of which is
supervision of the Director of Finance and all his staff, who provide financial
services to the Homes. He also serves as the executive officer for the California
Masonic Foundation, and this year, due to a reorganization resulting from the
dissolution of Nob Hill Masonic Management, assumed a new role as Chief
Executive Officer of the California Masonic Memorial Temple.
The Grand Secretary also handles specific administrative matters which
were once the province of Grand Lodge committees. When the Charters
Committee was dissolved he became responsible for new lodges seeking a
charter, and for lodges which are consolidating. When the Masonic Service
Bureau Committee was abolished, he became responsible for the operation of all
the service bureaus in the jurisdiction – including direct operation of the Bay
Area Masonic Service Bureau. In addition, he plays a major role in Masonic
trials, handling the preparation of Masonic charges, assisting the Grand Master
with the appointment of trial masters, and serving as the administrative arm of
the Trial Review Committee. Since there were almost fifty trials at one stage or
another in the last Grand Lodge Year, the responsibility of the Grand Secretary
for this operation was significant.
At the request of the Grand Master, I am deferring a report on the Theft
of the Grand Lodge Officer Jewels since the President of the California Masonic
Memorial Temple will make a complete report on the subject later today.
The Grand Secretary is responsible for the foreign relations of Grand
Lodge, including all correspondence with other jurisdictions around the world,
and recommendations to the Grand Master pertaining to issues of recognition.

PROCEEDINGS OF THE

114

As such he handles the matters that were once the responsibility of the Grand
Lodge Committee on Foreign Relations.
Last year this Grand Lodge approved the extension of recognition to
more than twenty Prince Hall Grand Lodges. In accordance with our law, we
had to first secure the permission of the Grand Lodge in the jurisdiction with
which we are presently in fraternal relations. Letters were sent to jurisdictions
which have already recognized the Prince Hall Grand Lodge within their
respective jurisdictions, and by August of this year unanimous consent for
recognition had been received. We then sent letters to the Prince Hall Grand
Lodge of each jurisdiction offering to enter into mutual fraternal relations. As
expected, this process will take time because each jurisdiction has to follow the
recognition procedures incorporated in their own Masonic law. As of this date,
one jurisdiction – Nebraska – has responded in the affirmative. I will therefore
recommend that mutual recognition between the Prince Hall Grand Lodge of
Nebraska and the Grand Lodge of California be approved.
It is a tradition for our Grand Lodge to extend recognition to new grand
lodges recommended by the Commission on Recognition of the Conference of
Grand Masters of Masons in North America. Based on their recommendations
at the 2005 Conference in Calgary, Alberta, Canada, I recommend that the
following Grand Lodges be recognized by this jurisdiction, which, including the
Prince Hall Grand Lodge of Nebraska, are as follows:
•
•
•

The Most Worshipful Prince Hall Grand Lodge of Nebraska.
The Grand Lodge of A. F. & A. M. of Latvia
The Grand Lodge of Malta

Most Worshipful, I move that recognition be extended to the above.
Motion Adopted.
Two lodges served “under dispensation” this past year. Dispensations
were granted to Ararat Lodge “UD” and to Panamericana Lodge “UD” by Most
Worshipful Howard D. Kirkpatrick in September, 2004. Sufficient time has
elapsed for these lodges to prove that they are capable of functioning as regular
Masonic lodges. Ararat Lodge is capable of conferring the Three Degrees of
Ancient Craft Masonry in English, although they hope to eventually confer the
degrees in Armenian. Panamericana Lodge is capable of conferring the Three
Degrees of Ancient Craft Masonry in Spanish, using our Standard Ritual in
translation. I therefore recommend that a charter be granted to each of these
lodges as follows:

GRAND LODGE F.& A.M. OF CALIFORNIA
•
•

115

Ararat Lodge No. 848
Panamericana Lodge No. 849

Motion Adopted.
This concludes my report as Grand Secretary. There are several
appendices to this report which are printed in the Proceedings, and approval of
them is incorporated by reference.
Respectfully submitted,
John L. Cooper III, Grand Secretary

PROCEEDINGS OF THE
Lodge Consolidations

All requirements of the law with respect to consolidation of Lodges
having been complied with, authorization is requested to properly endorse the
charters of these Lodges as follows:
Anaheim Azure Lodge No. 207 and A.M. Daylight Lodge No. 625,
effective November 1, 2004, to be known as Anaheim Lodge No. 207.
Oceanside Lodge No. 381 and San Dieguito Lodge No. 775, effective
November 1, 2004, to be known as Oceanside-San Dieguito Lodge No. 381.
King David’s Lodge No. 209 and Estero Lodge No. 719, effective
January 1, 2005, to be known as King David’s Lodge No. 209.
Stanislaus Lodge No. 206 and Modesto Lodge No. 675, effective
January 1, 2005, to be known as Modesto Lodge No. 206.
Seafaring Lodge No. 604 and Mar Vista Lodge No. 820, effective July
1, 2005, to be known as Newport Mesa Lodge No. 604.
Georgetown Lodge No. 25 and Hiram-Tahoe Lodge No. 43, effective
July 1, 2005, to be known as Hiram Lodge No. 25.
Brotherhood Lodge No. 370 and Columbia Lodge No. 388, effective
August 1, 2005, to be known as Columbia-Brotherhood Lodge No. 370.
Elsinore Lodge No. 289 and Perris Lodge No. 411, effective
September 1, 2005, to be known as Menifee Valley Lodge No. 289.

APPENDIX F

GRAND LODGE F.& A.M. OF CALIFORNIA

137

REPORT OF THE GRAND LECTURER
To the Most Worshipful Grand Lodge, F.& A.M. of California:
Brethren, as I stand before you, I do so with a great sense of humility
and pride for having the honor and privilege to serve you as your Grand
Lecturer.
In accordance with the California Masonic Code, I submit the
following report.
The quality of our ritual remains the highest priority of our efforts to
teach the membership the values and lessons of our great Fraternity. Ritual
review and instruction at Inspector Conferences, Division Schools of Instruction
for the Inspectors, Ritual Seminars for Officer Coaches and Ritual Seminars for
Master Masons have been held throughout the year. Requests for more Ritual
Seminars are echoed in many parts of this jurisdiction. Most of those seminars
have been conducted on weekends and arranging dates that do not conflict with
other activities is often difficult. Unfortunately, it has not been possible to reach
out to all areas of the jurisdiction on an annual basis. Therefore, in some cases,
the ritual quality for some Lodges is not what it should be. The use of the
cipher ritual and its supplement are valued aids, but do not fulfill the
exemplification requirements. In some Lodges, the quality of their degree work
is an indication of the need for additional instruction. Helping to correct Lodge
administrative problems has consumed much time that might otherwise have
been dedicated to instructing the lessons and values of Masonry. We must
continue to think positively and be exemplary in all our actions.
Quoting from a previous Grand Lecturer’s annual report:
“The Ritual is the foundation upon which Masonry stands and without
it our Fraternity would be as an empty shell, devoid of its strength and beauty
and entirely incapable of teaching to its followers the arts and sciences of the
Craft. Perfection of the Ritual and the proper rendition thereof is a subject,
worthy of much time and effort on the part of any Mason.”
During the past four years, your Grand Lecturer and the Ritual
Committee have sought out many avenues to effectively instruct our ritual and
create more interest within our membership.
The encouragement of personal pride and dedication to do the very best
that one can do, is instilled in the minds of those who are willing to apply
themselves. Lack of self-confidence overshadows the capabilities of those who
require that special coaching that is not always present. Some Lodges have not
followed the requirements of the California Masonic Code for Candidates’
proficiencies and education. The Inspectors continue to monitor those situations
and advise them of the proper procedures. This lack of attention to the

138

PROCEEDINGS OF THE

requirements set by you at previous Annual Communications would indicate
that more time should be devoted to Candidate’s Coaching instruction and
education.
This year, a test program of Ritual Competition was conducted in the
Senior Deacon’s work of the Middle Chamber Lecture. Of our 101 Masonic
Districts, 63 registered competitors and of those, 151 Lodges were represented
and 121 actually competed. Judging was based upon three categories: correct
words, floor work and presentation, which included pronunciation and
enunciation.
District, regional and division competitions were held throughout the
jurisdiction. The final jurisdiction or state competition was held in Lemoore on
August 6, 2005 with the five Division finalists competing for the jurisdiction or
state finalist. Each received appropriate recognition for their individual
dedication and ritualistic abilities at this Annual Communication.
The efforts of the Assistant Grand Lecturers continue to be most
commendable. Each has a heavy responsibility to bear, with their respective
Divisions, and as a team they still accomplish those assignments given them.
My sincere thanks go to each of them and also to the Inspectors, who deserve
far more credit than what is usually given them as they serve as instructors and
counselors and endeavor to contribute a level of peace and harmony to their
Lodges.
I am thankful for the cooperation and assistance of the Grand Secretary
and his staff, and in conclusion, I want to express my sincere appreciation to
Most Worshipful David R. Doan and his wife, Teri, to have had the opportunity
to serve in his Grand Lodge family. The leadership, friendship and memories
will remain forever and I “Thank You”.
To you, my Brethren, thank you for granting me this opportunity to
serve as your Grand Lecturer.
Respectfully submitted.
Harold A. Macy, Grand Lecturer

APPENDIX G

GRAND LODGE F.& A.M. OF CALIFORNIA

141

GRAND CHAPLAIN’S MESSAGE
by The Very Reverend Reuben B. Zari
Good afternoon. Before I proceed to share my short reflection today, I
would like to thank our Grand Master, the Most Worshipful David R. Doan for
his discretion and courage for appointing me as Grand Chaplain this Masonic
Year. Right from the very beginning, I was inspired to carry out my duties,
knowing full well that I have big shoes to fill, because our Grand Master
himself, as we all know, is a former Grand Chaplain.
I had the opportunity to hear him one morning during the breakfast
devotional at our junior wardens retreat; not only did he speak with “divine”
authority that morning, but spoke with what it seemed like a higher authority by
showing up in a well pressed police commander’s uniform, reminding everyone
of the commotions that had occurred in the hotel during the previous night! He
did look quiet impressive that morning!
Nonetheless, on a more serious note our Grand Master has been our
guiding Light this year. His leadership and dedication has inspired us all to
achieve the goal he set out to do this year – sharing the light of Freemasonry to
our communities.
So today, through God’s grace, I would like to share a reflection on our
Grand Master’s theme this year – Freemasonry: Values that brighten our
Communities – Share the Light!
Light, in masonry, is a symbol of knowledge. It is our desire, as
members of the family of freemasonry to be well informed about our teachings
in Masonry and therefore strive to have further light about the values of our
fraternity.
We derive our masonic values from the lessons we learn form our
rituals and degrees. They are taught through symbols and stories well calculated
to make it easier for us to learn and understand their meaning. Take for
example the Level and the Plumb. These two are instruments used in the
construction of structures or buildings where the level is used to prove
horizontals and the plumb to try perpendiculars. The Reverend Joseph Fort
Newton, a wonderful gifted Mason and respected author wrote what these two
symbols represent. The level, he writes, teaches unity in diversity and equality
in difference while the plumb is a symbol of rectitude of integrity of life, and
that uprightness of moral character, which makes a good and just man. Values
that will surely brighten a community!
Our travels these past months to Rio Linda, Vista, Coachella, Madera,
Visalia, Lake Elsinore, Elk Grove, Ceres and other parts of our great state to
conduct cornerstone ceremonies in dedicating new school buildings, affirm our
fraternity’s support and commitment to public education. By supporting our

142

PROCEEDINGS OF THE

public schools, we assure ourselves of a peaceful and loving nation where
children can learn the values of respect and genuine love for our way of life.
We thank our brethren who live in those communities who are active in their
school boards and who made themselves count in the development of our young
people.
Now as we believe in the light or knowledge of our fraternity, surely,
there must be a source of a brighter light that sparks a light within all of us.
In our beloved fraternity of Freemasonry, we all believe in a God that
lives in our hearts. The only question that was required of us to answer when
we first joined our fraternity was if we believed in a Supreme Being. We may
call Him by different names because we belong to different religions but in the
final analysis, we all pray and focus to the One God – the Father of All. God
has given us a great gift represented by the Holy Writings, which adorns our
Masonic altars, which we are taught as one of the Great Lights in Masonry. In
it, it tells us that God is our source of Light and He is our everlasting Father
from the beginning of creation. It is in this gift that Masonry encourages us to
strengthen our faith and govern our morality. Truly, God is Light and we must
share His Light!
Our theme reminds me of a story. Once upon a time, in a land far
away, a lighthouse sat on a rocky shore that helped ships get through in the dark
safely without hitting any big nasty rocks. One day the lighthouse keeper
became sick and a temporary substitute was put in charge of the lighthouse.
One night while he was there tending the lighthouse, a big storm arrived which
kicked up sand and branches and all kinds of things flying around in the wind.
The temporary lighthouse keeper got out a big piece of canvas and covered up
the lantern so it would not get wet or damaged in the storm. That night ships
blew upon the rocks and sank.
It sounds like a silly story doesn’t it? I mean – who lights a lamp and
then puts it under a bowl to cover it? In our Holy Writings, we are told that:
“You are the light of the world. A city built on a hill cannot be hid. No one
after lighting a lamp puts it under the bushel basket, but on the lamp stand, and
it gives light to all in the house. In the same way, let your light shine before
others, so that they may see your good works and give glory to your Father in
heaven."
Think about it for a minute – all of us today are told: "You are the light
of the world". What an incredible thing that is! What an incredible gift!
Somehow because of how God has made us, we are made into a light for the
world. That is the beautiful realty of what God does to us. In fact it is the most
important thing that God does through us, when we live by our faith.

GRAND LODGE F.& A.M. OF CALIFORNIA

143

Think of the people who have touched you, the people who first spoke
to your yearning heart, and the people who first, because of their love and care
for you, inspired you to believe in the God of Love.
Did they not light up your life? Did they not penetrate and change
your lives with their care, concern and honesty?
The divine is a light within us. And faith is the match that sets it
glowing. As members of our Masonic family, we should always seek further
Light in Masonry under the Light of God.
Yes, my brothers and sisters: We are the light of the world. And what
in all this does our Supreme Master ask of us? The One who puts his light into
us?
Our God asks very little. All God asks is that we follow him faithfully
as we can.
The simple fact is this: God makes us the light of this world and that
light is not meant to be looked at, it is not meant to be covered and treasured
like some precious object. It is given so that people might see what is around
them and walk in safety; it is meant to penetrate and overcome darkness.
We, in our fraternity, can stand up for our faith in our God, our
Supreme Grand Master and Creator of the Universe and we can stand upon our
faith in God.
And when we do so – stand up and stand on – when we believe and act
in faith, then we will be full of light and power and will be beacons in dark
places in our community and the world.
We have God's word on it; that word by which we were made and by
which all life is given.
In my travels this past year I had the opportunity to travel by plane and
one of the more interesting times to fly is during the early evening. As our
plane climbs to a prescribed altitude, it was always an interest of mine to look
out the window and see the ground below. Looking down I saw lights from
homes and streets, spreading out as far as to the foothills where new
subdivisions are being built. From this vantage point I could clearly see how
many more people are coming to our great state and as our Grand Master has
said in the beginning of his year we ought to take this as a challenge to spread
the light of Masonry to these new places.
I leave you with a passage from the Holy Writings, which should be
very familiar to us members of our family of Freemasonry. It is taken form the
Book of Romans Chapter 12 verses 9 to 18 from the African American Jubilee
Edition, modern day English version, which we can use as a guide when
spreading the Light of Masonry. And it says:

PROCEEDINGS OF THE

144

Be sincere in your love for others.
Hate everything that is evil and hold tight to everything that is
good.
Love each other as brothers and sisters and honor others more
than you do yourself.
Never give up.
Eagerly follow the Holy Spirit and serve the Lord.
Let your hope make you glad.
Be patient in time of trouble and never stop praying.
Take care of God’s needy people and welcome strangers into
your home.
Ask God to bless everyone who mistreats you.
Ask God to bless them and not to curse them.
When others are happy, be happy with them, and when they
are sad, be sad.
Be friendly with everyone.
Don’t be proud and feel you are smarter than others.
Make friends with ordinary people.
Don’t mistreat someone who has mistreated you.
But try to earn the respect of others, and do your best to live in
peace with everyone.
Simple words to live by, simple words to remember. However,
sometimes the simple things that we ought to do are the things we neglect to do.
Let your light shine, so people may see that you are indeed part of the
people of God. Let your light shine so that they may see your good works, as
members of the family of Freemasonry. May the God who is the source of our
light be upon us all. Share your Light!
Let us pray.
Our dear Heavenly Father, You are truly the God of Light and
for that we ask You to continually bless us with Your light
that we may see what is true and do what is right. May our
faith in You give the spark that lights a warm relationship
with You that we may see You in the face of our brothers and
sisters as we go out into the world to accomplish the tasks that
will give honor to your Name.
Amen.

REPORT OF THE GRAND ORATOR
“On Constancy”
Most Worshipful Grand Master, Past Grand Masters, Distinguished
and Honored Guests, Grand Lodge Officers, my Brethren all:
“When I have sometimes observed Men of Wit and Learning, in Spite
of their excellent natural and acquired Qualifications, fail of obtaining that
Regard and Esteem with Mankind, which their Inferiors in point of
Understanding frequently arrive at, I have, upon a slight Reflection, been apt to
think, that it was owing to the ill Judgment, Malice, or Envy of their
Acquaintance: But of late two or three flagrant Instances of this kind have put
me upon thinking and deliberating more maturely, and I find within the
Compass of my Observation the greatest part of those fine Men have been
ruined for want of CONSTANCY, a Virtue never too highly prized, and whose
true Worth is by few rightly understood.
“Without Steadiness or Perseverance no Virtue can long subsist; and
however honest and well-meaning a Man’s Principles may be, the Want of this
is sufficient to render them ineffectual, and useless to himself or others.”
The first American, our Worthy Brother Ben Franklin, made this
observation in 1734, when American Freemasonry was in its infancy. Now, in
the twenty-first century, is constancy a virtue worthy of our attention?
Is our belief in brotherly love, relief, and truth all that is required?
Or, must we constantly practice all Masonic virtues to prove their
meaning and value to the world?
Is constancy the virtue that reinforces commitment to our primary
values?
This year, your Grand Lodge officers performed our ancient, symbolic
cornerstone-laying ceremony a number of times for new schools. Five new
schools were dedicated in one ceremony in Elk Grove. Unique is the policy in
Elk Grove to name schools for living individuals, with a history of community
service.
One such honored individual is Mr. Edward Harris, Jr., a retired
chemist. His twenty-one years of service as a school board member pointed him
out as an example to the students; a middle school now carries his name
proudly. At the ceremony, Mr. Harris remarked, “My name is on that school.
That means I have to be good, every day, for the rest of my life!” An example
of constancy, my brothers, worthy of all imitation!
Traveling up and down this great state over the past year, we observed
many Lodges, both young and old, filled with our brothers, both young and old.

148

PROCEEDINGS OF THE

In each, one can observe enthusiasm for our Craft, meaningful activities for the
Family of Freemasonry, and service to their community. In all, the constant
display of our values and teachings. Constancy in word and action!
Freemasonry, as we know it, has spanned four different centuries. Our
ethics, morals, and lessons are as valuable in this age of electronic
superhighways as in the age of stone highways.
Our brothers of the “Greatest Generation” recognized our fraternal
values as a cornerstone in their lives. Having fulfilled their destiny to ensure the
freedom of our country, this band of wartime brothers, became a band of
fraternal brothers. We enjoyed the largest growth period in our history, and our
membership numbers swelled as never before, or since.
Sadly however, in ever-increasing numbers, these brothers are being
called to that house not made with hands; a journey my father has already made.
What made our Fraternity strong in the 1950s and 1960s? Was it really
our membership roll of two-hundred thousand?
Or, was the real strength of California Freemasonry the daily practice
and example of our values, by each member, in all our towns and cities?
To all these brothers, we owe two debts of gratitude.
Constancy in service to country.
Constancy in service to our Fraternity.
I submit to you that each of them has given full measure.
Ah, we baby boomers! Growing up with two superpowers glaring
across a tense world, we sought constancy in our own way. Yes, we distrusted
people over thirty. We involved ourselves in political actions and protests. We
were, by some accounts, an angry generation.
Some of us, however, did notice the constancy of virtue displayed by
our fathers; we recognized the source as this Fraternity. Some of us joined in
our twenties, some in our thirties. Others of my age, unfortunately, are only
now discovering the wealth of teachings in our Craft. I must admit that we
boomers have not done our share to maintain our Fraternity at the level left by
our fathers.
Can we now be the model for our sons and grandsons?
What shall become our legacy?
Our numbers are now below seventy thousand. At what point will we
pass the mantel of leadership?
Some think fifty thousand; others forty thousand.

GRAND LODGE F.& A.M. OF CALIFORNIA

149

Regardless of the final number, my brothers, will you practice daily our
values, as our fathers before us?
Constancy is the imperative, and thankfully, knows no size limitation.
The Millennial Generation is now coming of age. They have grown up
with political constancy in America.
Viet Nam was before their time.
President Nixon resigned before their decade.
Perhaps, some remember the speeches of the “Great Communicator,”
President Reagan, and sensed constancy in world order; the Iron Curtain fell in
shambles.
These young men are now beginning to join our Lodges. They see a
constancy displayed by their father, or grandfather, and cherish that virtue.
They are happy to listen to the words of senior members of a Lodge.
They are becoming active members, and are assuming line positions.
They will make fine Lodge masters, and thus model their own
constancy to others.
Can they fill every vacancy by our older members leaving us? No, but
I have faith that the good men of their generation will do their part. They will
portray constancy as a virtue, and their legacy.
Your Grand Lodge leadership has adopted a strategic plan, to align our
mission of Masonic teaching; our core values: tolerance, freedom, philanthropy,
family, personal growth, ethics; our vision of member vitality, lodge prosperity,
and community awareness. This plan will, over the coming decade, ensure a
constancy of purpose to guide our Craft.
Our leaders have also included a prime directive, that our programs,
membership development, and training require quality standards that are
independent of our membership size. The content must include value that
cannot be obtained from industry or academic sources. This training will not
just be free, it will be priceless.
I offer for your consideration, that as our strategic plan unfolds, the
training of future leaders must be of highest priority. Good men and true will
continue knocking on the door of Freemasonry, with a desire to learn leadership
skills; skills with a cornerstone of Masonic values. These same skills and
knowledge necessary for the strength of our Fraternity will, more importantly,
be applicable in business, community service, and government affairs.
We cannot lessen our duty to provide our communities, and our nation,
with skillful leaders; this has been an American Masonic constant since our

PROCEEDINGS OF THE

150

Boston brethren, not so long ago, held a small tea party in honor of King
George.
Over the past several years, my predecessors spoke to this issue of
decline in membership in our jurisdiction.
They spoke of a call to action.
That one Mason can make a difference.
Tell a friend about our Craft.
To pass it on.
That education is the key.
Ask what you can do for your Fraternity.
To each of you who heeded these calls, I say well done!
Your individual and collective deeds have manifested a new
groundswell of interest in this Fraternity. The number of applications to join in
our mysteries is showing a positive trend. While not the only metric of our
Fraternity’s health, it is nonetheless a harbinger of the future.
It is often said there is no such thing as bad publicity. The recent
motion picture National Treasure captivated young and old, Mason and nonMason. It was certainly fanciful in the depiction of a treasure left in America by
the Knights Templar. It also used our fraternal symbols as secret keys to the
treasure; it created a renewed interest in our Craft by all ages.
In print media, U.S. News and World Report showcased the
involvement by our Masonic brothers in the formation of this country. None
less than our Brother and President George Washington stood on the cover, in
Masonic regalia. The article referred to scholarly sources, both Masonic and
academic, and highlighted our Fraternity as an interesting subject for further
understanding by the general public.
Today, I ask for your continued good work:
To speak openly of our values and teachings.
To encourage good men, young or old, to join and become active
members.
For you to be an active member.
We must capitalize on the current interest in our Fraternity, in our
values.
We must be constant in our display of ethics, morals, and teachings.

GRAND LODGE F.& A.M. OF CALIFORNIA

151

Our Worthy Brother Ben also noted, “…for a Man who has no End in
View, no Design to pursue, is like an irresolute Master of a Ship at Sea, that can
fix upon no one Port to steer her to, and consequently can call not one Wind
favourable to his Wishes…”
Brethren, California Freemasonry offer a design in moral life, for each
of us, to purse each day;
To better mankind is the great end we have in view.
Our Masonic Lighthouse shines as a beacon of hope across the Great
State of California!
Will you make a difference?
Will you pass your legacy to the next generation?
Will you practice constancy, in word and action, as your personal
virtue?
Will you, each day, brighten a part of your community?
Brethren of California Freemasonry, will you share the light for our
future?
Respectfully submitted,
John F. Lowe, Grand Orator
