Board Meetings
The Board continues a system of holding meetings of the full Board of
Trustees on alternating months with the meetings of key committees. The full
Board meetings were held on the first Wednesday in the months of November,
January, March, July, and September, alternating between Union City and
Covina. In the alternating months of February, April, and August, meetings of
the key Committees of the Board were held, including Strategic Planning and
Finance. The Admissions Committee also met on a monthly basis. In May, the
Board and Committee meetings were held in Union City in conjunction with the
Grand Master’s reception. Other Committees, Medical Advisory Committee,
Board Development Committee and Scholarship Committee, met at least
quarterly or as needed. In addition, the Board held its annual two day strategic
planning retreat in March, 2005.
Admission Committee
By Gary Yanes, Chairman
The Admission Review Program of the Board is a carefully developed
analysis and evaluation process that continues to be improved and refined to
meet the evolving needs of our Applicants, as well as the Homes Staff and the
Board.
The interview process and on site evaluation is done at the same time
the applicant visits one of the two facilities. A Staff report on this overnight
visit, together with the Medical Director’s comments on his/her review of the
applicant’s medical history and data, is then added to the application
information. The staff then develops an executive summary on the applicants
and presents that to the Admission Committee for review and approval.
Anytime the Staff recommends against admission the full application is given to
the Admissions Committee for detailed review and discussion during the
Admissions Committee meeting. Any application that is denied requires the
confirmation of the full Board.
Prior to the regular Admission Committee meeting, held on the second
Tuesday of each month, the six Trustees, who form the Admission Committee,
receive and carefully study the admission summary for each prospective resident
or client of the Masonic Homes of California.. The Trustees review and
evaluate the applications and accompanying data, and discuss their viewpoint
during the Admissions Committee meeting. The Home’s Executive Staff attend
Admissions Committee meetings to answer questions and provide additional

GRAND LODGE F.& A.M. OF CALIFORNIA

233

information to members of the committee. The recommendations and
comments of the committee are presented to the full Board for confirmation.
The number of applications considered by the Admission Committee
for couples, individuals and children continues to vary from month to month.
From July 1, 2004 to June 30, 2005, 62 residents were admitted (57 on partial
assignment of assets and 5 on fee for Service). Union City currently serves 266
residents (85 on full assignment of assets, 166 on partial assignment of assets,
and 15 on fee for service); Covina currently serves 74 senior residents (15 on
full assignment of assets, 56 on partial assignment of assets and 3 on monthly
fee). For this time period, there were 73 applications completed by our brothers,
widows, and mothers.
The Masonic Outreach Services program (formerly known as NonResident Assistance program) continues to grow and is an effective program in
assisting our eligible Brethren and their families or widows. This program
provides the necessary support, financial, housing and/or care management
assistance to applicants who are in need of this type of assistance to remain in
their present community or residence. The Masonic Outreach Service program
currently serves 94 clients. This year the committee reviewed and approved 31
applications for this program. Members of the staff monitor these individuals on
a regular basis to ensure that their needs are met.
During fiscal year 2004-2005 the average cost per resident day by level
of care was as follows: Independent Living $106 per day, Assisted Living $159
per day, Skilled Nursing Care $233 per day, and MOS $71 per day.
The Covina facility provides hospice care for its terminally ill
residents, thus giving its residents a palliative care option previously only
available through the placement in a skilled nursing setting. The Union City
facility provides specialty care to residents with memory impairment in a
customized dementia care unit on campus.
During the past year, the Children’s program completed a major
strategic update. The board adopted a new philosophy of care, quality
outcomes, and admission criteria. An Alumni and Family Resource Center was
established to support the alumni, reunited families, and children who do not
meet the residential admission criteria. The therapeutic services and counseling
services were also expanded and a new behavioral program was introduced.
Based on the new admission criteria, children and grandchildren of Master
Masons in good standing in need of care or who are abandoned, abused, and
neglected are given first priority. If space is available admission consideration
is given to non- Masonic children, who are in need of care or who are
abandoned, abused, and neglected; and who have a high potential for self
sufficiency, academic success, post graduate readiness, emotional and social
well-being, family reunification, and good health.
All children will meet

234

PROCEEDINGS OF THE

criteria that focus on age, behavioral needs, mental disorder diagnosis, family
involvement, academic ability, and voluntary placement.
In 2005, the Children’s Program served a monthly census of 34
children; and was accredited by the California Alliance of Children and Family
Services.
I sincerely thank the other Trustee members of the Admission
Committee who labor long and hard over the applications. They are Michael
Day, Albert Gates, Richard Hopper, Phillip McAllister, Ken Nagle, and Michael
Neben. I also extend my appreciation to the Executive Staff of Masonic Homes
and their staff.
Board Development Committee
by Kenneth G. Nagel, Chairman
The Board Development Committee has responsibility for the annual
self-evaluation by the Board of Trustees of their own performance, for the
annual evaluation of the Executive Vice President, for identifying potential new
Board members and making recommendations on Board appointments to the
Grand Master and for Board education and enrichment planning. In addition,
this year the Board Development Committee took on the additional
responsibility of overseeing a compensation analysis for senior management and
for the formulation of a system of measuring management and organizational
performance as it relates to compensation.
The Board Development Committee met on three occasions during FY
2005. They administered and presented to the Board the results of the annual
board self-evaluation. In almost all areas the composite evaluation score far
exceeded a 3.0 on a scale of 1-4. There were recommendations for
improvement of the effectiveness of Board deliberations based on the survey,
including increasing the lead time in mailing out agenda materials in advance of
meetings and offering additional orientation on the financial statements for
those trustees who felt they could benefit from such review.
The Board Development Committee oversaw the interviews of several
prospective Board members, making recommendations on two new trustees to
the Grand Master to start terms beginning October, 2005.
The Board Development Committee worked with an outside
compensation consultant, James Rodeghero, PhD. to conduct a survey regarding
the appropriateness of the levels of our executive compensation and to redesign
our annual Goals & Metrics process a more precise and meaningful measure of
staff performance for purposes of incentive compensation.
Regular
compensation surveys are required under the Non-Profit Integrity Act to which
the Masonic Homes of California are subject. Based upon the results of the
compensation survey conducted in June 2005 under the Committee’s direction,

GRAND LODGE F.& A.M. OF CALIFORNIA

235

the Board of Trustees is confident that our current executive compensation
levels fall within the range of comparable market practices and support the
Masonic Homes of California affirmation of compliance with IRS and
California guidelines for reasonableness of executive compensation in taxexempt entities.
Children’s Committee
By Melvyn B. Stein, Chairman
In November 2004, the Children’s Program Committee was established
as a standing committee of Masonic Homes of CA. The purpose of the
committee is to provide oversight to the structure and effectiveness of the
Children’s Program. The committee reviews the quality of care and program
outcomes, incident reports and safety and wellness reports, as well as
administers the Masonic Homes of CA Scholarship Program in accordance with
the trust funds received.
Prior to establishing the Children’s Program Committee, a special
Children’s Task Force Committee of the Board of Trustees worked together
with staff to complete an extensive strategic update of the Children’s Program.
The overriding goal was to assure that our programs and services meet the
diverse needs of our Masonic families and children, and represent the standards
of excellence, best practices and outcomes consistent with our Masonic core
values. Through a strategic analysis the program’s internal strengths and
weaknesses were examined and the industry’s best practices were researched. A
needs analysis of the fraternity and surrounding community was also completed
to determine the unmet needs of children and families at risk.
With the support of the Board of Trustees a new philosophy of care,
program quality outcomes, and admission criteria was adopted. A Masonic
Family and Alumni Resource Center was created to provide a continuum of care
and services beyond the existing residential programs to support the alumni,
reunited families and families who do not meet our admission criteria. The new
philosophy of care is entitled ”Circle of Courage” which promotes belonging
and healthy relationships, teaches the mastery of skills, develops self
sufficiency, and encourages the virtue of generosity so that children can be
contributing members of society.

236

PROCEEDINGS OF THE

Based on the new admission criteria, children and grandchildren of
Master Masons in good standing in need of care or who are abandoned, abused,
and neglected are given first priority. If space is available admission
consideration is given to non- Masonic children, who are in need of care or who
are abandoned, abused, and neglected; and who have a high potential for self
sufficiency, academic success, post graduate readiness, emotional and social
well-being, family reunification, and good health.
All children will meet
criteria that focus on age, behavioral needs, mental disorder diagnosis, family
involvement, academic ability, and voluntary placement.
To support the implementation of the Children’s Program, a new
infrastructure was put in place including several new staff, extensive training,
new policies and procedures and a new program statement.
Additionally
several new program quality outcomes were established including health and
safety, academics, family reunifications, alumni success, and volunteerism.
Finally a new strength based behavioral program was introduced and the
therapeutic counseling services were expanded.
In 2005 the Children’s Program Committee awarded two academic
scholarships and a single grant for housing assistance. The following is a
summary of the awards;
The first alumnus successfully completed his sophomore year at Fresno
State University. He was previously awarded $15,100 for the summer and 200405 academic year. The Scholarship Committee granted up to $13,210 of
ongoing tuition and housing support for the 2005-06 academic year.
The second Alumnus completed a part time course of study for the
academic year 2004-05 at Citrus College in Azusa, CA and was awarded
$6,387. The Scholarship Committee denied a subsequent request for an
academic scholarship for 2005-06. However the committee approved a monthly
housing allowance of $250, for a period of one year, while the alumnus
maintains full-time employment. A vocational scholarship will be considered
for the next academic year if the alumnus is able to successfully maintain fulltime employment.
The Scholarship Committee granted $6,060 of tuition and housing
support to a high school senior of the Masonic Homes children’s community.
The scholarship covers the academic year 2005-06 for Rio Hondo Community
College, in Whittier, CA.

Finance Committee
By Larry L. Adamson, Chairman
The role of the Finance Committee is to review and recommend
approval to the Board of Trustees the operating and capital budgets. The
Committee also monitors actual performance against those approved budgets.
The Committee also reviews recommendations from the Investment Committee
of Grand Lodge regarding investment strategies for Home’s funds as well as
recommendation for spending guidelines that establish parameters for operating
and capital expenditures.
The Finance Committee reviews and approves the reports of the
external auditors as well as special internal audits that are conducted from time
to time. From these audits, the Committee works with management to develop
appropriate action plans. In collaboration with the Strategic Planning
Committee, the Finance Committee oversees long term financial planning as
well as the review of financial analysis relating to new programs, operational
initiatives and capital projects. The Committee advises the Board as to the
appropriate sources of funding for all proposed projects and programs.
The Masonic Homes of California revenue ended fiscal year 2005 with
a favorable budget variance of just over 2%, resulting from better than expected
resident related revenue. Expenses also ended the year favorable to budget just
under 2%. This performance required careful management of expenses in light
of ever increasing costs associated with employee benefits, insurance coverage

238

PROCEEDINGS OF THE

and increased demand for Masonic Outreach Services (MOS). We are pleased
to report that overall expenses for the Masonic Homes has remained relatively
flat over the past 5 years, despite high inflation in health care related expenses,
compared to the general rate of inflation.
For the past several years, operating expenditures of the Homes have
been guided by spending parameters set as a percentage of a five-year rolling
average of the investment balances of the general and endowment funds. This
approach insures operating expense will be somewhat buffered from significant
year-to-year market swings while protecting the integrity of the fund balances
for future generations of residents. The positive performance of the financial
markets during the past year has helped to mitigate some of the expected decline
in this spending calculation. However, continuing high inflation in the health
care industry is putting a strain on our ability to operate the Homes within this
guideline. The Committee, Board and Staff continue to evaluate the operations
of the Homes to increase efficiencies, without sacrificing our high level of care
while maintaining the fiscal health and strength of the Homes. In addition, the
Committee, Board and Staff continue to explore opportunities to diversify the
revenue sources of the Homes to better protect the operation for future
generations.
The Finance Committee reviewed regular reports from the Grand
Treasurer. These reports include quarterly investment performance reports,
monthly operating statements, quarterly capital project reviews and various
others. PricewaterhouseCoopers (PwC) completed the annual external audit for
the fiscal year ended June 30, 2004. PwC gave the Homes a “clean” audit by
issuing an unqualified opinion on the completeness and accuracy of the
financial reports.
The Finance Committee worked with management to develop the fiscal
year 2006 budget, based on the direction that management use a “zero-based”
approach in formulating their budget recommendations. In the face of ever
rising costs, management proposed, the Finance Committee recommended, and
the Board approved a budget which actually came in below the 5% spending
guideline.
The Finance Committee also worked with management to develop a
capital project budget to assure the Homes remain a well maintained facility and
protect the comfort and safety of our residents. The Homes continues to fund
the Capital Reserve fund from annual depreciation through the operating
budget. This fund is used to pay for capital projects and to build a reserve for
future needs. The Committee along with management developed a fiscal year
2006 capital budget which will allow the balance in this fund to grow for future
large capital needs.
Along with the Strategic Planning Committee, Board and Staff, the
Finance Committee is reviewing and monitoring several possible major strategic

GRAND LODGE F.& A.M. OF CALIFORNIA

239

initiatives. These initiatives include the creation of additional independent
living units at both the Union City and Covina campuses. These additional units
will allow the Homes to serve more members of the fraternity and to reach out
to Masons previously underserved by our current facilities. In addition, these
developments will allow the Homes to more effectively and efficiently use the
current infrastructure of the Homes.
Special projects reviewed by the Committee included the renovation
and upgrade to the main entrance at the Union City campus. This project not
only improved the appearance of the entrance, increased security of our
residents, but also will decrease our security costs.
The Committee and Staff, working with the Grand Secretary of the
Grand Lodge, completed an extensive review of our employee benefit plans,
including cost structures. In order to better manage employee time away from
work, and to contain the increasing costs of necessary replacement labor, the
employee vacation/sick time system was converted to paid time off (PTO). This
system also allows the employee more flexibility to use and manage their time
off. The Committee continues to close review and monitor other employee
benefit programs to mitigate the increasing costs of these programs.
The Committee, in conjunction with Staff and external consultants,
completed an extensive review of the salary structure of the Homes. The salary
for all positions throughout the organization were reviewed and compared to
external market data. This information was invaluable in crafting a salary
structure which is competitive in our markets. In this way, we can retain the
best employees possible to provide high quality care to our residents, while
protecting the charitable dollars entrusted to the Homes.
To promote transparency and openness of the financial operations of
the Homes, and to comply with State regulations, through Grand Lodge, the
Board approved the creation of a separate Audit Committee for the Homes.
This Committee will work with the external, independent auditors to ensure the
financial reports of the Homes is accurate, timely and complies with all
necessary requirements. In addition, the Audit Committee recommends to the
Grand Master (per CMC section 7030) the retention of those external auditors.
The Audit Committee recommended, and the Finance Committee and Board of
Trustees concurred, that the external audit firm of Moss Adams be retained for
the fiscal year 2005 audits. The Audit Committee serves as the Audit
Committee for the Grand Lodge and all Masonic Related Entities. This allows
the Committee to gain experience and knowledge of all operations and provide a
more knowledgeable level of over site.
The Committee, along with the Insurance Committee of Grand Lodge,
completed a review of the insurance coverage of the Homes. Through the
efforts of the insurance broker for the Grand Lodge, Marsh Risk and Insurance

240

PROCEEDINGS OF THE

Services, the costs of insurance coverage remained relatively flat, while
increasing the level coverage.
The Committee and Staff continued to work on the comprehensive
review and evaluation of the cost structure of the Homes, without sacrificing the
high quality care affording to our residents. This evaluation also allowed us to
benchmark our costs against other, similar facilities to better understand our
operations and areas which may need further evaluation. These analyses help us
to better understand the costs of providing our high level of care and personal
service and to determine effective strategies to mitigate the ever rising costs.
The Committee continues to closely monitor the MOS program. This
program provides services to Masons and their families in their homes and
communities. The program has been extremely successful. In the fiscal year
ended June 30, 2005, the expenses associated with this program increase more
than 18% over the prior year. The Committee continues to work with Staff to
develop and implement strategies to manage the growth in this program within
the constraints of the budget and out financial resources. However, as noted
previously, in spite of this increase in MOS expenses, the overall expenses of
the Homes ended the year under budget due to effective cost management.

Medical Advisory Committee
by Mark N. Gibson, Chairman
The Medical Advisory Committee oversees the evaluation and
continuous improvement of the quality of services provided to our Masonic
Home residents. In addition, the Committee monitors compliance with
appropriate licensure and accreditation programs and measures resident
satisfaction.
This year the committee continued to monitor a number of quality
indicators, comparing them both to internal trends, and where available, external
industry standards. These include: quarterly tracking of statistics related to skin
condition, weight loss, falls and psychotic drug usage.
The Committee consolidated the adult statistics with a standardization
of the indicators for both campuses. Indicators fell within the desired limits
when compared with like communities. Further plans are to continue tracking
quality indicators to optimize care given to our residents and for the residents to
maintain the highest level of health possible.
Resident’s Activities of Daily Living (ADL’s) and Instrumental
Activities of Daily Living (IADL’s) are being tracked to evaluate the level and
amount of services provided on both campuses.
Reports from the Wellness Programs on both campuses shows that
each program has influenced residents to make good health choices and to
further improve their ability to live independently for as long as possible. The
Union City campus is doing this with presentations and informative talks by a
variety of health professionals. Both Union City and Covina are promoting their
new fitness centers and encouraging all residents to participate in the fitness
programs.
Changes in the operations of the Health Center on the Union City
campus were discussed. Primary care physicians are now seeing residents at
their office rather than on campus. This is to further align the two campuses’
services and to eliminate the social visiting many residents practiced when the
Health Center was open for drop-in treatments.
Our Medical Director, Dr. Sheila Raumer, has worked closely with
both campuses to assist with reducing the usage of over-the-counter and
prescription drugs. She has also monitored infection control trends, quality
assurance efforts and has been involved in the review of future medical research
projects.
A bequest from the Bloom Family Trust was sent to the Homes of
approx of $180K, restricted to research uses. The Committee will report to the
board on possible research projects that we can participate in, probably
associated with geriatrics, for board information and approval.

PROCEEDINGS OF THE

242

Modified Qualify Indicators to track clinical medical visits to
determine causes. We now compare ourselves against averages in California and
the United States for comparisons.
Union City is preparing for Medicare Part B implementation in January
’06 for Pharmacy Services. New Psychotropic committee was fully established
and the number of residents on antipsychotic agents has declined.

Strategic Planning Committee
by George L., Chairman
This past year has been a particularly busy and challenging one for the
Strategic Planning Committee of the Masonic Homes of California. We have
overseen our operations and maintained our facilities while developing plans to
protect our assets and our future. We have striven to be the best possible
stewards of the resources entrusted to our care and balance the needs of today’s
residents with the emerging needs of tomorrow’s residents.

GRAND LODGE F.& A.M. OF CALIFORNIA

243

Membership Needs
The concept of the Masonic Homes of California as the “unifying
symbol of relief” appears as the opening note in our vision statement. What
does this mean? Over 100 years ago our forefathers built the Masonic Homes of
California to act as the organized arm of relief for our fraternity. In effect, they
created a ‘safety net’ for our fraternal family.
This safety net fills the gaps in the social support network –
particularly for the elderly and children. We conduct ongoing research into the
health and well-being of our members and the services available in California to
identify the holes in available services. For instance, we expanded our Masonic
Outreach Services (MOS) program by over 30% this year because we know this
program provides needed support without which many members of our fraternal
family would be living in deplorable and unsafe conditions that demean their
humanity.
Our goal is to ensure that all members of our fraternal family have
access to the services they need – even if we don’t provide them. We do this by
conducting surveys and focus groups with our members to learn more about
their retirement needs and preferences and match these against the available
services throughout California. In doing so, we have identified a real shortage
of affordable residential services for the middle-class senior. There are many
high-end communities for seniors with sufficient resources and there are state
and federally subsidized programs for the poor – but it is the middle-class senior
who doesn’t have sufficient choice or access to needed services.
We have spent the past three years considering options to address this
unmet need without putting any further demands on our charitable dollar.
Given our financial constraints, we can consider new programs only if they are
break-even or provide a new source of revenue to fund our charitable mission.
Challenges
Our focus on improving our communications with the membership and
outreach to our vulnerable members has proven to be successful. We now have
a waiting list of between 1-3 years, depending on the level of service needed.
In response to an increasing demand for Assisted Living (AL), in
August of this year we launched the new centralized intake department. The
purpose of this new department is to offer our membership a single point of
contact for all calls for Masonic assistance. This department now triages and
screens callers and ensures that those in need of immediate service are served
immediately. We may not be able to be the actual provider of services to our
constituents but we do all we can to ensure they have access to needed services.

PROCEEDINGS OF THE

244

This demand for our services is occurring at a time when we are facing
significant economic pressures. Currently, the Masonic Homes of California
relies heavily on our investments to fund operations. Over the past five years,
we have reduced the percentage of operational expenses financed by the
proceeds of the investment earnings from 90% to 75%. Annual contributions
from the Craft represent another $600,000 - $800,000. New funding sources,
including resident fees and income, make up the difference.
Given the demand and unmet need for services in our membership, we
are exploring every possible alternative to service reductions.
Strategic Response
In the face of increasing need and an uncertain economic forecast, this
committee has spent almost three years and countless hours researching,
discussing and considering ways to respond to these challenges.
We are working closely with the Department of Philanthropy to
improve the support from the members. Our belief is that if the members knew
the value of the services and support provided through the Masonic Homes, they
would give gladly and generously. As such, our commitment is to do more to
“tell the story” of the work being carried out by the Homes and the impact on
the individuals we serve. The Strategic Planning Committee oversees the
communications activities, which this year included:
¾

Launched new user-friendly, information rich website – first time
applicants can initiate applications on line

¾

Direct mail to leadership, all master masons, two widows mailing,
monthly mailings to entered apprenticed masons, fraternal support
services committee and lodge ambassadors

¾

Personal phone calls to all lodge masters in California

¾

60+ presentations across the state

¾

Developed all new collateral materials

¾

Ongoing articles for trestleboards

¾

Relaunched News from Quarries

¾

Articles in every issue of CA Freemason

¾

Community health fair in Paradise Park

We have completed a thorough review of all aspects of our operations
and benchmarked ourselves against the industry. We challenge ourselves to

GRAND LODGE F.& A.M. OF CALIFORNIA

245

operate as efficiently as possible, while maintaining the highest standards of
quality and resident satisfaction. We also initiated discussions with UCSF for
some exciting quality initiatives and signed the “Quality First” covenant of the
American Association of Homes and Services for the Aging (AAHSA).
This year a special task force developed and implemented a new
philosophy of care, program model and outcome measures for the Children’s
program on the Covina campus. The goal was to expand our focus from the
child in need to the family in crisis. We have now developed programs that
focus on the whole family with a goal toward reunification or permanent
placement whenever possible. I am proud to announce that in June of this year,
our program was fully accredited through the California Alliance of Children
and Family Services. I thank the members of this committee who staffed the
children’s task force and commend them on a job well done.
New Senior Housing
After reviewing our members needs and identifying the holes in the
social safety net, our efforts for the past few years has been on identifying
initiatives that meet the following criteria:
¾

Diversify our service offering to a wider segment of the fraternity

¾

Create a new revenue streams to help fund our charitable mission
and reduce our reliance on investment earnings

¾

Improve our utilization of existing resources (Land, administrative
overhead, etc)

As a result of our planning and researching, we are working on a
proposal to create two new senior communities on our existing campuses. As
envisioned, they will provide new options for seniors interested in living in an
affordable retirement community with members of our fraternal family. We are
excited by the potential for these new communities as they will serve an unmet
need, help fund our charitable mission, make effective use of our land and
spread our infrastructure over a larger resident base. We will not be using any
charitable dollars to fund these new developments and will go forward only if
we find sufficient interest in the fraternity.
Conclusion
In closing, the Strategic Planning Committee and its Chairman would
like to acknowledge the leadership and support of the entire Board of Trustees
of the Masonic Homes and its President, Allen B. Gresham, PGM. Together we

PROCEEDINGS OF THE

246

are committed to upholding the standards of excellence demanded of us by the
guiding principles of freemasonry and to ensure our services are delivered in a
manner befitting our founding ideals. It is an honor and a privilege to be a
member of this Board.

REPORT OF THE EXECUTIVE VICE PRESIDENT
September 2005
The management team of the Masonic Homes of California is
comprised of the Executive Director, Union City, Robert Fallon, the Executive
Director, Covina, John Howl, and the Director of Program Development and
Masonic Outreach Services, Steffani Kizziar. Each of these managers report to
the Executive Vice President. In addition, the Homes contracts with Grand
Lodge Corporate Services to provide a variety of staff support services,
including Finance, Human Resources, Information Technology, Membership
Services and the Office of Philanthropy. The Director of Finance is Mark
Koenig. The Director of the Office of Philanthropy for Masonic Services is
Doug Ismail. The Director of Human Resources for the Grand Lodge/Homes is
a shared position held by Wendy Riley and Linda Shomaker. The Director of
Information Technology is John Wickey. The corporate services staff also
function as members of the Masonic Homes of California executive team.
The following sections highlight the achievements during the last year.
More detailed summaries of operational milestones are included in the reports of
the Executive Directors of the two campuses.
Services and Programs
In an effort to continue to serve as a partner with individual Masons
and lodges for the provision of relief to the membership, the Board and
management team has made over 60 presentations to fraternal groups over the
year and provided regular updates on programs and services in The California
Freemason and News from the Quarries publications. Our user-friendly, data
rich website has been updated and now can be used for on-line applications for
MOS or admission. Personal phone calls have been made to every lodge master
inquiring as to their interest in scheduling a lodge presentation. Direct mail
information was also sent on a regular basis to leadership, master masons,
widows and new entered apprentice Masons in our membership data base.
Invariably these communication activities resulted in an increased rate of
applications for admission to the Homes and for assistance through the Masonic
Outreach Services (MOS) program.
Increasing Resident Acuity/ Waiting List Management
This year management recognized a trend of increasing acuity, or
higher level of need, on the part of both our current residents and applicants on
our waiting list. This presented two potential challenges: a need for increased
staffing and expense within our two Homes and the likelihood that many

248

PROCEEDINGS OF THE

applicants on the waiting lists might have to wait for much needed services. To
address this issue, each of the Homes established target ratios for the proportion
of independent living to assisted living residents. In addition, the MOS staff
collaborated closely with the Homes staff to determine if assisted living
applicants on the waiting list could be placed in high quality community based
facilities either as a temporary measure till space in the Homes became available
or as a permanent placement.
Through the creation of a centralized intake function, all inquiries for
admission or assistance are now quickly assessed and triaged so that their needs
can be met expediently. By working in partnership with preferred providers in
our communities, including skilled nursing facilities, PACE providers and
dementia facilities, the Homes has been able to extend our mission to more
recipients than if we tried to provide all services within our own facilities. Since
institutional dementia care tends to be the most expensive form of long term
placement, we have made every effort to place as many applicants in need of
this type of care in our Traditions unit in Union City.
During the past year the number of clients supported financially
through our MOS program has again increased by 30%. In addition we provide
case management support and information referral to many of members of the
fraternal family who may not need direct financial assistance.
Quality of Care
Evaluation of resident and applicant satisfaction with our services is an
important indicator to the Board and management of the effectiveness and
quality of our services. In 2005 we transitioned to a new outside survey firm,
Halloran Consulting, to conduct our resident satisfaction surveys. Halloran is
noteworthy for maintaining some of the finest non-profit Continuous Care
Retirement Communities (CCRC’s) in the country among their clients. Our
satisfaction results, in which over 85% of residents participated compare very
favorably with this data base of outstanding facilities and showed improvement
over prior years in many areas of service, including quality of food service,
satisfaction with recreation programs and resident participation in facility
planning and decision-making. Management presents the results of satisfaction
surveys to the Board and the residents themselves, using the findings to plan our
priorities for continued enhancement of our programs and operations.
We have launched new fitness and exercise programs at both
campuses, equipping our facilities with apparatus designed to safely meet the
exercise needs of a senior population. We have also begun discussions with the
University of California San Francisco regarding collaborating on two research
projects should add to the body of research on issues of health and care of the
elderly as well as benefit our own residents.

GRAND LODGE F.& A.M. OF CALIFORNIA

249

Our surveys of new residents regarding satisfaction with our admission
process showed an overall 94% satisfaction (up from 89% in 2004) at Union
City and 92% (up from 91%) at Covina. A new “user friendly” application
allows our fraternal family to apply either for MOS or admission using the same
document.
Children’s Program
This year we implemented the recommendations of strategic plan for
our children’s program developed by a Board task force last year. We have
transitioned from the Boy’s and Girl’s Town program to a new model of care
that places more emphasis on therapy and counseling in addition to the behavior
modification that was stressed in the past. We have set goals for increased
family participation in counseling and therapy and reunification of children with
their families when the family dynamics permit. Family reunification rates have
increased and we place emphasis on follow-up with program alumni to ensure a
successful reunification and development of the life skills necessary to make a
successful transition outside the Home. We plan to track this data, including the
academic and job success of our alumni as a measurement of the effectiveness
of the program.
Employee Relations and Organizational Development
In health care organizations, our workforce is our most important asset.
Over 50% of our expenses are labor related. Given the challenges most long
term care providers have in employee recruitment and retention, we are proud
that our employee turnover rates are remarkably low. Position descriptions have
been updated and workplace safety audits have been completed at both
campuses, including ergonomics evaluation and work station redesign.
While we need to be a competitive employer in the design of our wage
and benefit packages, we also know successful employee relations rest with
effective and prepared managers and clear-cut expectations and performance
standards for employees. We have invested time in developing the skills of our
managers in problem resolution, communication and effective supervision.
This year all of our middle managers completed a “360°” performance analysis
by their supervisor, peers and direct reports that gives them feedback on their
personal strengths and weaknesses as a leader. Based on this feedback, each
manager in collaboration with their supervisor can establish their own personal
development plan objectives.
We know that our employees value their benefit packages, which in
our case are quite competitive in our industry, as much or more than their
compensation levels. This year we converted to a paid time off system that
affords employees more flexibility in scheduling their time off while reducing

250

PROCEEDINGS OF THE

potential misuse of sick time. Our open enrollment benefits presentations for
employees now include presentations by human resources personnel as well as
representatives from our benefit carriers. Overall employee satisfaction surveys
confirmed an overall satisfaction of 81% (up from 63%) at Covina and 84% (up
from 65%) at Union City.
Financial Results
For the past several years, the Board and management have striven to
keep our operational expenses within 5% of the past 5-year averages of the
balance of our investment portfolio. Like many non-profit organizations, we
feel this kind of prudent usage of our investment proceeds ensures that we will
have adequate resources to care for our current as well as future residents and
clients, especially in light of the aging demographics of our membership.
We were very pleased that for several years in a row, we not only
produced a budget that fell below the 5% spending target, but we achieved a
$1,363,000 favorable variance in net expenses compared to the budget. Each
year we have saved the favorable variances between the targeted 5% and actual
net spending in a strategic reserve fund. The strategic reserve fund will then
provide seed capitalization in the form of loans to finance some of the key
strategic initiatives described in the President’s report and other committee
reports. As of June 30, 2005, the balance of the Strategic Reserve Fund was
$2.8 million. Our capitalization strategy ensures that our new strategic
initiatives, which will of course themselves contribute to the extension of our
charitable mission, will in no way be financed out of endowment funds.
A key mechanism for keeping our spending below budgeted levels has
been on-going scrutiny of costs, exploration of system improvement
opportunities and maximization of outside revenue sources. We continue to
compare our costs for various elements of services (e.g. food costs per day, or
nursing care costs per day) compare favorably with available industry
benchmarks. In addition, in an effort to offset the dramatic increase in the
number of MOS clients served, we were able to reduce the cost of care for each
MOS client by 24% through careful management of our client’s budgets and
accessing available reimbursement.
This year we completed an analysis of the projected life and
replacement needs for all our capital resources. This capital reserve analysis
will allow us to project our long-term capital needs, which is then incorporated
annually into our budget. We also set aside the full cash value of our
depreciation each year into a Capital Reserve account, in order to fund future
capital needs. This is a very fiscally prudent practice which is not able to be
accomplished in many health care organizations. The balance of our capital
reserve account as of June 30, 2005 was $12 million.

GRAND LODGE F.& A.M. OF CALIFORNIA

251

Strategic Initiatives
Summaries of the key strategic initiatives pursued by management over
the last year are included in the report of the President and the Strategic
Planning Committee.
In closing, the management team wants to thank and acknowledge our
outstanding members of the Board of Trustees, who give countless hours of
their time and creative thinking for the benefit of our residents, clients and
fraternal constituents.
Respectfully submitted,
Deborah E. Stebbins, Executive Vice President

REPORT OF THE EXECUTIVE DIRECTOR
MASONIC HOME, UNION CITY
2004 - 2005
On behalf of the residents and staff of the Masonic Home in Union
City, it is my pleasure to provide this annual report. The Masonic Home has
just completed another successful year as we continue to stay true to our
Mission and continue to serve those in need. The services we provide the
residents continue to be second to none while at the same time keeping costs
down and preserving the Endowment Fund. We completed the year by being
under budget by over 2% while at the same time we showed strong
improvement in our resident surveys for all departments and in overall
satisfaction categories.
This past year was an important one for me personally because I
became a Master Mason in September of 2004. Coincidently, during the same
week I was raised, my wife gave birth to our daughter just 48 hours before my
third degree. I am now a member of Live Oak Lodge No. 61 in Oakland,
California and the proud father of Zoe Tuolumne Fallon.
A key achievement during this past year was the initial plans to
renovate both the 3rd and 4th floors of the Lorber Skilled Nursing Facility. Our
plans are to expand the dining room, improve the nurse call system and remove
one resident room to act as a lounge area for the residents on that floor. We
want to improve the lighting in the activity rooms and the hallways, shrink the
nursing station and make it wheelchair accessible, open up and improve the
bathing area, provide new wardrobe furnishings for all residents' rooms, carpet
the hallways and common areas and add fresh paint and art work throughout.
Since we are just initiating this project we do not anticipate this renovation to
begin any earlier than the summer of 2006.
Another key component for the Masonic Home is the creation of the
initial plans to expand our campus to include more housing for independent
seniors. This decision came about through extensive review by the executive
management team and with consultation from the Board of Trustees. We
studied the services we provide here at the home and compared our ratios to
other successful retirement communities throughout the country. It has become
clear that the Home is very weighted towards caring for frail seniors and that
our health care facilities were overbuilt for the size of community we presently
have. In the continuing care retirement industry, it is common to see 70% of
your population as independent living residents, 15% as assisted living residents
and 15% as skilled nursing residents. Presently the Masonic Home at Union
City has 34% independent residents, 41% assisted living residents, (which
includes our dementia unit) and 25% skilled nursing residents who reside on

GRAND LODGE F.& A.M. OF CALIFORNIA

253

only two of the three floors in Lorber, (the second floor is presently
unoccupied).
This addition of independent units will be able to provide more housing
for members of the Masonic family while keeping our operating costs increases
to a minimum. We plan to use our existing management team to oversee this
addition which will in turn provide significant cost efficiencies to the campus as
a whole. We only need to hire additional dining services, housekeeping and
maintenance staff for these addition buildings. With these additional units the
Masonic Home will be able to achieve cost efficiencies in staffing, health care
services and programs while at the same time improve the events on campus,
provide needed housing for the middle income Mason and bring our housing
ratios more in line with the other successful retirement communities.
The Masonic Home has continued its charity work with the local
community. Each year the staff and residents volunteer their services and
monies to support the less fortunate residents from the city of Union City. We
have resident volunteers who work with the local police and fire departments
and Washington Hospital. We also provide support for five poor families by
purchasing their Christmas gifts and helping them enjoy their holiday season.
We contribute to the Washington Hospital Tree of Angles which supports those
in the community who need financial support. We provide scholarships towards
higher learning for graduates of Logan High School. And our biggest event is
the Annual Tree Lighting ceremony on the grounds of the Home. Over one
thousand city residents, officials, employees, local schools and performance
groups come to the home for this grand event. It is also a wonderful opportunity
for our residents to show off their beautiful Home to the surrounding
community.
This past year the Masonic Home was the lucky recipient of Grand
Master David Doan’s Special Project. The Grand Master’s Special Project was
to improve and enhance the services we presently provide to residents of the
Home who have Alzheimers and/or dementia and who reside in our Traditions
unit. The project included the creation of memory window boxes, an enhance
wondering garden, the creation of wheelchair accessible planting boxes and the
construction of a 30’ diameter (720 sq ft.) yurt to be built as a new activity
center. This new center will be used year round for all types of activities and it
will also house the fountain and fish pond that had not been operational for
many years. This enclosed environment will provide the residents the sensation
of still being outside in a natural environment while eliminating the impact that
uncomfortable weather has on our frail seniors. The residents are very excited
about this new addition and have expressed many thanks to the Masons who
contributed to this year’s Grand Master’s special project.
Notable residents’ achievements this year included the birthdays of the
following residents:

PROCEEDINGS OF THE

REPORT OF THE EXECUTIVE DIRECTOR
MASONIC HOME, COVINA
2004 – 2005
On behalf of the residents and staff of the Masonic Homes at Covina,
we are pleased to submit the following annual report highlighting the
accomplishments and challenges our campus experienced this past year. As you
review our report, we hope you will sense our commitment to excellence and
innovation as we strive to provide outstanding care and services to our residents
and families. Additionally we would like to express our sincere appreciation to
our Board of Trustees and the Masonic Lodges for their dedicated leadership
and faithful support of our community.
During the past year we served a senior population which averaged 73
residents and represented a resident mix of 60% independent living, 35%
assisted living, and 5% skilled nursing. Over this same period, we experienced
14 admissions, 3 transfers to skilled nursing, and 11 deaths. Also during this
time our waiting list expanded to 49 prospective residents, representing 38
households. Our average waiting period for admission was approximately 18
months. In the event that a prospective resident needed transitional support
prior to admission, we were fortunate to access the outstanding support of our
Masonic Outreach Program.
As we serve the changing physical and emotional needs of our
residents, our senior community continues to offer a “continuing care” model of
care that provides a full spectrum of care and services. Each resident is treated
with respect and dignity as they “age in place” in a safe and caring environment.
At this time, given the relatively small senior population we serve, our skilled
nursing services are provided off campus. As our residents health care needs
increase and they require skilled nursing services, they are given the choice to
receive services at our Union City campus or to transfer to a high quality
nursing home in our surrounding community. We have established several
preferred relationships with area high quality non-profit skilled nursing facilities
that meet our standards of excellence. Should a resident live off campus in a
preferred skilled nursing facility, we remain actively involved in the oversight
of their care.
In further focusing on the health and safety of our residents, we
established several quality indicators which we monitored over the past year.
These resident indicators included falls, weight changes, changes in activities of
daily living, and emergency room visits. Tracking this data was extremely
helpful in examining trends and measuring health changes. With this
information we were able to more effectively develop personalized care plans
for each resident. Additionally, we initiated more resident and family

GRAND LODGE F.& A.M. OF CALIFORNIA

261

participation in the care planning process through including them in our
interdisciplinary care planning meetings.
In April of 2005 we conducted our annual resident satisfaction survey
through Holleran Consulting, a professional research firm serving many of the
premier Continuing Care Retirement Communities across the country. For the
second year, our resident satisfaction mean scores were well above the national
benchmarks. With 100% of our residents participating in the survey, our Overall
Satisfaction mean scores were 93.8 in Independent Living and 92.8 in Assisted
Living. Both scores were above the national benchmarks by 7.1 and 3.8 points.
The survey included a total of 40 service factors representing every area of
service. Our service strengths included: friendliness and courtesy of staff,
cleanliness and maintenance, quality of housekeeping services, accommodation
of special dietary requests, effective management of changes, and opportunity
for resident input. The areas for improvement included: quality of spiritual
services, confidence in primary care physicians and specialty care providers,
quality of laundry services, and the temperature of food and variety of menus in
the assisted living dining program. Through resident feedback and input, we
have developed action plans and strategies to improve these service
opportunities. Our top performing scores were in activities and environmental
services; and the most improved areas of service included activities,
independent living dining, and transportation. As a team of staff we recognize
that we are shifting from a tradition healthcare base of service to one of
hospitality. Recognizing customer service is essential, we collaborated with our
Union City campus and with the assistance of a professional customer service
training firm, to design and implement our own Masonic Homes customer
service excellence program. The program reflects the Masonic values of
Brotherly Love, Relief and Truth and teaches critical customer service skills to
all staff. As an organization we pride ourselves on being an industry leader in
high quality services and care to senior adults.
Also this past year we completed several significant capital projects to
enhance our living environment and the quality of life for our residents. From
water heaters, boilers, and roof replacement, many of the projects were not
highly visible, but very much needed. One of our major projects included the
replacement of our stoves, ovens, steamers, and dishwasher in the main
production kitchen. This improvement has directly impacted the quality of our
meals and the sanitation of our kitchen. One of the measures of a high quality
organization is the annual percentage of funded depreciation. Not only do we
fund 100% of our annual deprecation, but we have forecasted our capital needs
for the next 30 years through a professional capital forecast and reserve
analysis. As a fraternity we have been blessed with beautiful grounds and
facilities which we must maintain.
One of the unique attributes of our community is that we serve both
seniors and children on a single campus. While the Masonic Homes at Covina

262

PROCEEDINGS OF THE

has served seniors for nearly 15 years, this past year we celebrated 90 years of
service to children, who experience abandonment, abuse and neglect. On
September 12 2004, we joined together more than 500 guests of the broader
Masonic family for our 90th anniversary. This special event was a time of
celebration and recognition of our long enduring mission that has provided
relief and care to children and families in need. Given the success of the event,
it was agreed that we would plan and host an annual Summer Festival to bring
together our Masonic Lodges and concordant Masonic bodies for a time of
fellowship and celebration. On June 12, 2005, we held our Second Annual
Summer Festival with more than 500 guests in attendance. It was an equally
successful event which was enjoyed by our residents and guests.
During the past year our Children’s Residential Program served 17
boys and 15 girls, with an average census of 32. Over this same period we
experienced 15 admissions, 16 discharges, and 1 graduation. Approximately
50% of our population has a Masonic affiliation through a family member, most
commonly a grandfather. Our average length of stay is approximately 2.5 years.
Additionally our newly established Masonic Alumni and Family Resource
Center served 59 families, who reunited with their children from our residential
program and/or did not meet our admission criteria. We also provided academic
scholarships to 3 alumni.
One of our major corporate initiatives this past year was the strategic
update of our Children’s Program. Our goal was to assure that our programs
and service meets the diverse needs of our Masonic families and children, and
represent the standards of excellence, best practices, and outcomes consistent
with our core Masonic values. Through a strategic analysis we examined our
internal strengths and weaknesses and researched the industry’s best residential
practices. We also conducted a needs analysis of our fraternal membership and
surrounding community to determine the unmet needs of children and families
at risk. Through the leadership of a newly established Children’s Program
Committee of the Board of Trustees, we developed a new philosophy of care,
program outcomes, admission criteria, and improved cost effectiveness. We
also created a Family and Alumni Resource Center to provide a continuum of
care and services beyond our residential program, for alumni, reunited families
and families who do meet our residential admission criteria. We adopted the
“Circle of Courage” philosophy of care which promotes belonging and healthy
relationships, teaches the mastery of skills, develops self sufficiency, and
encourages the virtue of generosity so that children can be contributing
members of society. Our new program outcomes focus on health and safety,
academics, family reunifications, alumni academics and job placement, and
volunteerism. We have also introduced a new strength based behavioral
program and expanded our therapeutic services. This major undertaking
required the revision of our operating policies and procedures, extensive staff
training and a passion for excellence. We are thrilled with the new vision of our

GRAND LODGE F.& A.M. OF CALIFORNIA

263

Children’s Program and the potential for great success in influencing and
shaping the lives of children and families for the future.
Finally, you will see a list of all the seniors and children we served this
past year. As a resident centered, mission driven community, we pledge to you
our dedication and commitment to carry out our mission of providing high
quality care to California Master Masons, their wives, widows and mothers as
well as to children with or without Masonic affiliation, with dignity and
compassion, within an atmosphere, based on Masonic tenets, that fulfill the
physical, social, spiritual and nurturing needs of our extended community.
Respectfully submitted
John E. Howl, Executive Director

GRAND LODGE F.& A.M. OF CALIFORNIA

269

REPORT OF THE TRUSTEES OF THE
CALIFORNIA MASONIC FOUNDATION
To the Most Worshipful Grand Lodge, F.& A.M. of California:
The Foundation has now completed its first year under the reorganized
structure reported to you last year. Under this plan, the Foundation works
closely with the California Masonic Memorial Temple association. In fact, the
Trustees of CMMT meet quarterly on a Tuesday evening and, since they also
serve as Trustees of the Foundation, they join with the other Foundation
Trustees the following morning for the quarterly Foundation meeting.
The CMMT now operates as a “supporting organization” to the
Foundation and performs the functions heretofore performed by Nob Hill
Masonic Management, Inc.
CMMT now transfers to the Foundation revenues from the operation of
the Nob Hill Masonic Center over and above those funds actually required by
CMMT for its ongoing operations, thereby providing additional funding for the
expanded work of the Foundation.
A summary of the work of the Foundation this past year is as follows:


The awarding of 37 new and renewal of 90 scholarships to deserving
high school graduating seniors and college students continuing their
pursuit of an undergraduate degree.



With the continuing cooperation of the Masonic Homes of California,
the Foundation sponsored four 3-day MSAP workshops and two 1-day
booster sessions with a total attendance of approximately 380 public
school teachers, administrators and staff.
In addition, the Foundation assisted Naval Lodge No. 87 of Vallejo
which sponsored an MSAP session in Vallejo for the teachers of
Solano County. Naval Lodge held fundraising events to generate
sufficient funds to support what resulted in our largest MSAP
workshop in history, with 110 faculty members being trained to
identify and intervene effectively with students at risk. Naval Lodge
should be proud of their efforts and the resulting honor they have
received from their community. This is an effort worthy of imitation,
my Brethren.



Funding of the California Teacher of the Year program by awarding
$1,000 to each of the five teachers selected annually by the State
Superintendent of Public Instruction. These awards are presented to
teachers in their own communities by a Foundation representative

PROCEEDINGS OF THE

270

usually at a school board meeting. This focuses attention on the local
Masonic Lodges.


Assisting the Leadership Training Program of each of our four
Masonic youth organizations through financial support.



The continued funding of the Child ID program which this year made
available, at no cost, a new computer control system for those Lodges
utilizing this important program.



The support of Masonic education by providing monetary support of
the Annual Symposium, the relocation of the Grand Lodge Museum
and Library, and by underwriting a portion of the cost of the 2005/06
Wardens Leadership Training. These were made possible through the
generous donations to our Annual Giving Campaign where the
Foundation was designated as a recipient and also through the
generosity of Brother Henry W. Coil.



Grants for schools sponsoring Cornerstone ceremonies were resumed
with a check for $1,000 being presented at each such ceremony.



Working with the trustees of CMMT, a large grant was made for the
remodeling of the third floor of this building providing a more efficient
environment in which our staff goes about the day-to-day operations of
our Grand Lodge. If you have not taken the time to see the changes for
yourself, I encourage you to do so.



Other historic sites around the state have been visited, and information
pertaining thereto has been developed. The Foundation has concluded
that one such site, the Old Masonic Hall at Plaza de Los Angeles,
which has not been occupied for some 11 years, should be eliminated
from Grand Lodge involvement. Since this is the former home of Los
Angeles Silver Trowel Lodge No. 42, they have been given the
opportunity to take over the facility if they so desire and they are
currently evaluating that possibility.

The work of the Foundation has grown greatly since its beginning
some 35 years ago and is well beyond the age of “the scholarship” entity. In
fact, I will say that the best years of the Foundation still lie in the future.
This important work is due to the dedication and hard work of each of
our Trustees and to them I shall be forever indebted. To you, Most Worshipful,
I say on behalf of all Trustees of the Foundation, thank you for the opportunity
to serve our Craft.

REPORT OF THE BOARD OF TRUSTEES
OF THE CALIFORNIA MASONIC MEMORIAL TEMPLE
To the Most Worshipful Grand Lodge, F. & A. M. of California:
The Trustees of the California Masonic Memorial Temple are pleased
to present the 50th annual report to the Grand Lodge on issues concerning the
condition and management of the physical facility as well as the financial
condition of our Memorial Temple. This report is an overview and abstract of
activities this year.
The dissolution of the Nob Hill Masonic Management Corporation
(NHMM) was completed on schedule and NHMM ceased doing business as of
September 30, 2004. The CMMT resumed its previous role of operating the
rental of the facility, but this time as a support organization of the California
Masonic Foundation.
Our five-year capital improvement plan is on schedule with the
completion of the third floor construction, which better utilizes unused square
feet with additional meeting, office, and storage space. Please visit the third
floor to see the improvements that have been made. The next major project will
be the elevator upgrade that will be completed during the FY 2005-06. This
project is projected to cost $275,000. Provided we have the revenue, the next
major project will be the repair and/or replacement of the auditorium seating.
A separate project requiring special funding is the restoration of the
Endomosaic Window in the lobby and replacement of the windscreen panel
outside. Emile Norman, the artist who created this mural, lives in Big Sur and is
helping with this project. The cost of this badly needed work is expected to cost
$400,000. Douglas Ismail, Director of Masonic Philanthropy, is working on a
special funding program for this expense.
Although started after the end of our 2005 FY the new Henry Wilson
Coil Masonic Library has just been completed. The library and museum
displays will occupy space on the mezzanine level of the Temple. Brother
Henry Coil Jr. has been most generous with his financial support of this as well
as the Endomosaic Mural project. Also, a special viewing place will be created
in this area for looking at and learning more about the mural.

272

PROCEEDINGS OF THE

You are invited to review the full and complete audited financials
included in the appendices of these proceedings. We are indebted to Director of
Finance Mark Koenig and his staff for their excellent handling of the accounting
matters and keeping the Board informed regularly on our financial condition.
Anyone visiting our facility, members and non-members alike, is
impressed with the outstanding condition and maintenance of the Temple. The
hard work of Terry Hobson, our Director of Operations in this area is greatly
appreciated. The first impression of potential renters is always good. This will
continue to contribute to favorable and multiple bookings.
Under the immediate direction of our Chief Operating Officer, Allan L.
Casalou, changes were made this year in our marketing strategy for the facility.
We booked over 300 event days in the building this fiscal year. A new sales
and marketing manager joined us in September. His primary role will be to
develop new facility renters and work closely with and under the immediate
direction of Allan.
The box office in the lobby was completely refurbished during the
year. With electronic ticket sales now being offered, we have a good on-going
source of revenue.
The Board also wishes to extend a sincere vote of thanks to our Chief
Executive Officer and Grand Secretary, John L. Cooper III, who continues to
get 26 hours out of every day. His advice and counsel continues to provide
guidance to the Board in a variety of ways.
Our Chief Operating Officer, Allan Casalou, should also be specially
recognized for his willingness to jump into the fray at times during the year with
strong direction and organization as well as burning the midnight oil when
needed. The Board thanks Allan for his many contributions.
Finally, and with a bit of sadness, we will bid farewell to our Grand
Treasurer, M∴W∴ Warren J. Blomseth, who has made countless contributions
to the well being and management of the CMMT. Most Worshipful Blomseth
has not only been valuable in his role as Chief Financial Officer of the
corporation, but a great idea person also. In retirement, his phone will probably
still be ringing.

THEFT OF GRAND LODGE OFFICER JEWELS
Report of David D. Johnson
President, Board of Trustees, California Masonic Memorial Temple
Some time between October 14, 2004, and June 2, 2005, the Grand
Lodge Officer Jewels, together with the Square and Compass used by Grand
Lodge during the Annual Communication, was stolen from a safe inside of the
walk-in vault in the Grand Lodge Office. The California Masonic Memorial
Temple is responsible for security of this building, and as such has assumed a
primary role in studying any holes in its existing security system which may
have led to the theft. It should be noted that the San Francisco Police
Department was immediately notified by the Grand Secretary when the theft
was discovered, and they are conducting a continuing investigation into the
theft. In addition, Grand Lodge has retained a private firm to assist with the
investigation.
The circumstances attending the theft are as follows. On October 14,
at the close of the last Annual Communication, the Grand Tiler placed a
briefcase containing the Grand Lodge Jewels in the safe in the walk-in vault on
the Third Floor of the Grand Lodge Office. The Jewels had been stored in this
location since the early 1990’s, and the procedures for locking them up at the
end of each day of the Annual Communication were followed. The Grand Tiler
has confirmed that he properly locked the safe on October 14.
On June 2, 2005, the Grand Secretary opened the safe in the vault to
check on the Grand Master’s Jewel which was to be used the following week
for a photograph to be taken of the Deputy Grand Master as Grand Master with
the Grand Master’s Jewel – something that is done every year. When the safe
was opened, the briefcase was missing. A thorough search of the vault ensued,
and then the San Francisco Police were contacted.
They responded

274

PROCEEDINGS OF THE

immediately, and the Burglary Detail of the San Francisco Police Department
subsequently conducted an in-depth investigation, including checking for
fingerprints. Unfortunately the age of the safe (it is approximately 100 years
old) meant that no fingerprints could be obtained. They also interviewed all
employees who had access to the vault and to the safe. Only four employees
had the combination to the safe at the time the theft was discovered, and each
affirmed that the combination was carefully protected from any unauthorized
access or use.
In addition, the electronic printout of access to the Third Floor through
use of electronic keys was scrutinized, covering the period of October 14
through June 2. Nothing out of the ordinary was discovered. Between the end
of October and February 3 the Grand Lodge Office staff did not occupy the
Third Floor because of remodeling. The vault was locked during construction
except for two occasions when large furniture was moved into the vault and the
subsequently moved out of the vault. A Grand Lodge Office staff person was
present on site both days, and did not observe anything unusual. However,
information on the moving company was furnished to the San Francisco Police
Department so that they could interview those workmen involved in the move.
As of this date there is no additional information from the police department on
this aspect of the investigation.
In 2003 the CMMT Board authorized the retention of a security
consultant who produced a security plan for the building. The recommendations
from this consultant are in the process of being implemented, and include the
addition of more security cameras on the premises, changing a cardkey access
system to the Third floor. There were two security cameras on the Third Floor
before remodeling began, which were reactivated after the construction was
completed. However, in accordance with the security plan and industry
standards, the video recordings were only preserved for four to six weeks before
the information is erased by subsequent re-recording. Thus there were no
videos available of the Third Floor except for six weeks before June 2. The
videos did not yield any pertinent information.
Access to the Third Floor is by cardkey only. Regular metal keys are
used for the three fire escapes, and only a limited number of keys are issued.
All were accounted for. In addition, access is obtained by a cardkey used in the
two elevators. Again, all cardkeys were accounted for, and – as stated above – a
scrutiny of the information stored on the computer about the use of the cardkeys
between October 14 and June 2 did not yield any useful leads.

GRAND LODGE F.& A.M. OF CALIFORNIA

275

The San Francisco Police Department continues to actively work on
this theft, and their efforts will be assisted by the private investigator hired by
Grand Lodge. All possible leads will be pursued. It should be noted that the
Grand Secretary and the Grand Tiler immediately volunteered to take a
polygraph test to confirm their version of events. Both passed, and were
removed from any active consideration by the police. Other polygraph tests of
people with access to the combination to the safe will be conducted in the future
by the police.
In an effort to strengthen security in the building, the CMMT Board at
its July meeting voted to retain another firm to conduct an updated security
study. That study has now been delivered to the CMMT, and we are proceeding
to implement some of the recommendations that were made. For example, even
though two cameras covered the Third Floor area where the vault is located,
there were no cameras in the vault itself, or outside the door of the vault. That
has now been remedied. In addition, backup of the video recordings are
preserved for a much longer period and a periodic inventory of the vault will be
conducted before the tapes are erased. The combinations of the safe and the
vault have been changed, with new procedures in place to ensure that access to
the combinations is much more restricted than before. Under the new plan only
the Grand Secretary has the combination to the safe, while the combination to
the vault has been entrusted only to two employees other than the Grand
Secretary. There are other recommendations which will be considered by the
board at its meeting later this month.
The Board of Trustees of the CMMT shares the concern of all the Craft
for this loss, and is determined to take whatever steps are necessary to protect
the valuable assets of Grand Lodge. We regret that the three layers of security
previously in place were obviously breached, and are committed to providing
whatever remedy we can to safeguard that which is entrusted to our care.

REPORT OF THE CALIFORNIA FREEMASON
MAGAZINE EDITOR-IN-CHIEF
To the Most Worshipful Grand Lodge, F. & A. M. of California:
The California Freemason is published quarterly and the content of
each issue is guided by a theme. The theme is communicated through several
articles that are related to one of seven sections of the magazine, which include:
In California, Lodge Spotlight, Masonic Education, Cover Story, Around the
World, History, and Masonic Homes.
In the 2004/2005 Grand Lodge year, four issues were published for a
total distribution of 326,200. Information about each issue is below.

276

Theme: Freemasonry & Hollywood

Theme: Youth & Leadership
Quantity for Distribution: 81,500

These: Freemasonry and the Community
Quantity for Distribution: 81,500
In April, we received notification from the Society of National
Association Publications (SNAP) that the California Freemason magazine was
awarded national honors in the SNAP 2005 Excel Awards.
SNAP is an association of more than 750 U.S. membership
organizations that publish magazines and newsletters. These organizations
represent a wide variety of trade and professional associations, charities, and
academic, cultural, and service organizations. Members include the American
Bar Association, Habitat for Humanity, the American Diabetes Association;
Jewish Women International, Rotary International, U.S. Golf Association,
Sigma Xi, and Penn State Alumni Association.
The Excel Awards recognize excellence in print and electronic
publishing. The top three publications are selected in several categories and
ranked with either a gold, silver, or bronze distinction – much like the
Olympics.
The California Freemason, Fall 2004 edition “Freemasonry on the
Frontier” received the bronze award in the Best Single Topic Issue category.
Many people contribute to the successful publication of the California
Freemason. I would like to extend my appreciation to the members of our
regular writing team including Richard Berman, Terry Mendez, Alison Steiner
Miller, Steffani Kizziar, and Ann Lyon Dudley. We are also pleased to have a
regular editorial from one of the members of the Executive Committee and each
month we look forward to an article of Masonic Education from Grand
Secretary John L. Cooper III. I would like to recognize Burritt Design for the
continued outstanding graphic design of the magazine. I also offer praise and
extend my gratitude to Kimberly Murawski who served as the Managing Editor

GRAND LODGE F.& A.M. OF CALIFORNIA

277

of the magazine and also coordinates the publishing, printing, and mailing
components of publication.
Finally, I thank Most Worshipful David R. Doan, Grand Master, for
the opportunity to lead and mange this important publication. It is indeed, a
privilege and an honor.
Respectfully submitted,
Allan L. Casalou, Editor in Chief
